Illuminate an isolated target in a uniform transparent host by a monochromatic plane wave of incident intensity \(I_0\). A far-away receiver collecting solid angle \(d\Omega\) measures scattered power \(dP_{\rm sca}\). Define \(d\sigma_{\rm sca}/d\Omega=(dP_{\rm sca}/d\Omega)/I_0\) and integrate over direction to obtain \(\sigma_{\rm sca}\). Dividing internally dissipated power by the same incident intensity defines \(\sigma_{\rm abs}\). Extinction is \(\sigma_{\rm ext}=\sigma_{\rm sca}+\sigma_{\rm abs}\) for a passive isolated object in a lossless surrounding medium. All three have dimensions of area, even for a particle that has no sharply defined physical footprint.

The geometrical area of an object and its cross section answer different questions. A transparent object much smaller than its wavelength may scatter only a small fraction of the power geometrically incident on its silhouette. A resonant dipole can draw energy from an effective area comparable to a wavelength squared, much larger than a nanoparticle's silhouette. The incident field and the disturbance it generates coexist in the forward direction. Removing a fixed area from a ray diagram cannot represent the interference between those fields.

Represent the incident polarization by a normalized complex vector \(\mathbf e_0\perp\widehat{\mathbf z}\). Define a vector scattering amplitude of dimensions length by \(\widetilde{\mathbf E}_{\rm sca}=E_0 e^{ikr}\mathbf f(\mathbf n)/r+O(r^{-2})\) for the same \(e^{-i\omega t}\) convention. The scattered wave is transverse to n; for a polarization-insensitive receiver \(d\sigma/d\Omega=|\mathbf f|^2\). With a receiving analyser \(\mathbf a\) only \(|\mathbf a^\dagger\mathbf f|^2\) is detected. Thus an intensity pattern alone loses information about phase and possibly polarization. The forward extinction will depend on one particular quadrature of that phase.

Derive the small sphere's polarizability from two boundary equations

In vacuum take a dielectric sphere of radius a with scalar relative permittivity \(\epsilon_r\) at the driving frequency. For \(ka\ll1\) and negligible retardation inside the sphere, solve the instantaneous-looking boundary problem for a phasor incident field \(E_0\hat z\). Axial symmetry and the degree-one angular dependence admit \(\Phi_{\rm in}=-C r\cos\theta\) and \(\Phi_{\rm out}=-E_0r\cos\theta+D\cos\theta/r^2\). At r=a continuity of tangential E is equivalently continuity of these potentials; continuity of normal D supplies the other equation. They give

\[C=E_0-D/a^3,\]
\[\epsilon_r C=E_0+2D/a^3,\]
\[D=a^3{\epsilon_r-1\over\epsilon_r+2}E_0.\]

Comparing the decaying term with a dipole potential gives \(p=4\pi\epsilon_0D\). Define the volume polarizability by \(p=\epsilon_0\alpha E_0\), so \(\alpha_0=4\pi a^3(\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\) in m³. Some conventions absorb \(\epsilon_0\) in \(\alpha\). Naming the convention avoids spurious permittivity factors when scattering formulas are converted between sources. At \(\epsilon_r\to1\) the particle becomes indistinguishable from its environment and the dipole vanishes. In the formal electrostatic conducting limit the coefficient tends toward \(4\pi a^3\).

Radiation from this induced electric dipole gives

\[\mathbf f(\mathbf n)={k^2\alpha\over4\pi}\,[\mathbf e_0-\mathbf n(\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf e_0)],\]
\[\sigma_{\rm sca}={k^4\over6\pi}|\alpha|^2 .\]

The projection shows why scattered light from unpolarized illumination becomes partially polarized at an angle: average the power of two orthogonal transverse incident polarizations. For observation angle \(\theta\) to the incident wavevector, the normalized angular factor is \((1+\cos^2\theta)/2\). At 90° an analyser can suppress the surviving polarization. The total cross section is independent of the orientation of a single linear polarization because the isotropic sphere can be rotated with that polarization.

Predict an absolute scale as well as a wavelength slope

For a transparent sphere \(a=20\) nm, index 1.50 so \(\epsilon_r=2.25\), and \(\lambda_0=600\) nm, the contrast factor is 0.2941 and \(ka=0.2094\). The quasistatic volume polarizability is \(2.96\times10^{-23}\) m³. Substitution gives \(\sigma_{\rm sca}\simeq5.6\times10^{-19}\) m² compared with geometric area \(1.26\times10^{-15}\) m². At otherwise fixed properties, halving radius divides \(|p|\) by eight and \(\sigma_{\rm sca}\) by 64. Changing wavelength from 600 to 500 nm instead increases the leading cross section by \((600/500)^4=2.074\). This scaling assumes that material dispersion and finite-size corrections have stayed sufficiently small.

For a dilute suspension with \(N=10^{16}\) particles/m³ path loss from independent scattering gives \(dI/dz=-N\sigma_{\rm ext}I\). Over 0.10 m at the 600 nm parameters, optical depth is about \(5.6\times10^{-4}\), or 0.056% collimated intensity loss. Collecting a wider transmitted cone can admit some of the scattered light and reduce that apparent extinction. At high density fields scattered by one particle drive others and positional correlations can alter angular scattering. The isolated-particle prediction and exponential independent-particle extrapolation should be tested separately.

Change particle radius and compare a calculated scattering cross section

Find an interference signal that survives at arbitrarily large distance

Write the total field outside the scatterer as incident plane wave plus outgoing spherical wave. The Poynting flux through a large sphere contains incident-only, scattered-only and cross terms. The net incident-only flux through a closed sphere vanishes. The scattered-only term tends to \(I_0\sigma_{\rm sca}\). The interference term accounts for the reduction of incident energy in the forward beam. To see its scaling, examine a narrow forward cap; in a scalar polarization-preserving calculation the relevant angular oscillation is \(e^{ikr(1-\cos\theta)}\). The interference field is order \(1/r\), but the integration area is order \(r^2\); oscillatory phases cancel over most directions and leave a forward contribution of finite order.

Near the cap \(\theta\ll1\), \(1-\cos\theta\simeq\theta^2/2\), \(d\Omega\simeq2\pi\theta d\theta\). Introduce a smooth angular acceptance equal to one near zero. The leading forward factor in the integral is \(2\pi\int_0^\infty \theta e^{ikr\theta^2/2}d\theta=2\pi i/(kr)\), with arbitrarily weak positive damping understood before taking the large-radius limit. Magnetic and electric interference terms have the same forward polarization and supply twice the real cross product in total flux. A backward stationary contribution is cancelled by the opposing propagation directions in the net flux. With the outgoing-wave convention this gives

\[\sigma_{\rm ext}={4\pi\over k}\Im[\mathbf e_0^*\cdot\mathbf f(\widehat{\mathbf z})].\]

This is the optical theorem. A forward detector sensitive only to \(|\mathbf f(0)|^2\) is insufficient; the unscattered incident field acts as a coherent reference that picks out the appropriate quadrature. Changing the scattering-amplitude convention changes corresponding signs. Inserting a known passive dipole is a quick way to verify the convention used in a computational implementation.

There is an equivalent small-particle derivation. The cycle-averaged work extracted from a prescribed incident electric field is \(\langle\mathbf E_{\rm inc}\cdot\dot{\mathbf p}\rangle=(\omega/2)\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde{\mathbf E}_{\rm inc}^*\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf p})\). Dividing by \(I_0=|E_0|^2/(2Z_0)\) gives \(\sigma_{\rm ext}=k\operatorname{Im}\alpha\). In the forward direction the dipole formula gives \(\mathbf e_0^*\cdot\mathbf f(0)=k^2\alpha/(4\pi)\), reproducing that result exactly. Comparing integrated scattered power with interference-derived extinction tests both amplitude normalization and global energy accounting.

Repair the apparent contradiction of a real static polarizability

A strictly real static \(\alpha_0\) gives positive dipole radiation in \(\sigma_{\rm sca}\), yet inserting that same real number into \(k\Im\alpha\) would give zero extinction. The two formulas then mix orders of approximation. Replacing the driving field by its electrostatic value determined the leading in-phase moment. The small imaginary part needed to supply radiated energy arises at the next electrodynamic order. For a lossless purely dipolar particle energy conservation requires \(\sigma_{\rm ext}=\sigma_{\rm sca}\), hence

\[\Im\alpha={k^3\over6\pi}|\alpha|^2,\]
\[\Im(1/\alpha)=-{k^3\over6\pi}.\]

Retaining the static inverse real part yields a simple radiation correction \(\alpha=[\alpha_0^{-1}-ik^3/(6\pi)]^{-1}\). For a real \(\alpha_0\) this expression obeys the lossless optical theorem. It captures the radiative imaginary correction while ignoring possible real dynamic depolarization and higher multipoles; it is not an exact all-size sphere solution. If the material itself absorbs, a passive \(\alpha_0\) contributes additional loss, and \(\sigma_{\rm abs}=k\Im\alpha-k^4|\alpha|^2/(6\pi)\) must remain nonnegative.

Define \(b=k^3\alpha/(6\pi)\). Passivity is \(\Im b\ge|b|^2\), which is the disk \((\Re b)^2+(\Im b-1/2)^2\le1/4\) in a complex-amplitude diagram. Points on the boundary are purely radiative dipoles; interior points have additional internal absorption. The largest possible \(|b|\) is one at \(b=i\), giving \(\sigma_{\rm sca}=6\pi/k^2=3\lambda^2/(2\pi)\). At that point the particle is lossless and extinction is all scattering. The largest absorption occurs instead at \(b=i/2\). There \(k\Im\alpha=3\pi/k^2\), scattering is \(1.5\pi/k^2\), and the difference \(1.5\pi/k^2\) is absorbed. The result applies to one ideal electric dipole channel with the stated normalization. Additional channels can contribute separately for a larger or more complex object.

Choose the detector geometry that answers the desired question

A narrow-aperture transmission experiment measures an extinction-like signal if it isolates the original mode. A large integrating sphere estimates total outgoing scattered flux over its acceptance and provides an independent \(\sigma_{\rm sca}\); a temperature-rise measurement estimates heat deposition. For the same incident beam, the combination should satisfy \(P_{\rm ext}=P_{\rm sca}+P_{\rm abs}\). Measuring extinction and assuming it is all absorption can exaggerate heating by orders of magnitude for transparent dielectric particles. Conversely reflection plus a small-angle detector may miss side-scattered light and turn that deficit into an apparent unexplained source term.

Suppose a calibrated intensity is \(2.0\times10^4\) W/m², transmission implies 60 pW extracted per particle, and integrated scattering gives 45 pW. The inferred cross sections are \(3.0\times10^{-15}\) and \(2.25\times10^{-15}\) m², leaving \(0.75\times10^{-15}\) m² for absorption and 15 pW thermal load. If each of the two power readings has independent 5 pW standard uncertainty, the difference has about 7.1 pW uncertainty. The inferred absorption is then much less certain in relative terms than either directly measured quantity. A negative fitted difference within that uncertainty should prompt an examination of collection angles and background subtraction rather than an immediate interpretation as optical gain.

How energy conservation fixes a scattering phase

The forward optical theorem can appear surprising because it relates a single angular amplitude to a sphere-integrated power. For a spherical scatterer this relationship can be seen one angular momentum channel at a time. Begin with a scalar plane wave scattering elastically from a spherically symmetric object in a lossless exterior. This scalar example exposes the mathematics; electromagnetic spherical waves have separate electric and magnetic polarisation families and start at angular momentum one. A scalar zeroth channel should never be inserted directly into a vector light-scattering calculation.

Outside a sphere containing the object, the radial equation for each Legendre component has independent incoming and outgoing spherical solutions. Define \(S_\ell\) as the ratio of outgoing to incoming wave amplitudes relative to its value for free propagation. The radial energy flux in a channel is proportional to the squared magnitude of the outgoing amplitude minus that of the incoming amplitude. For an elastic scatterer flux balance demands \(|S_\ell|=1\). Set \(S_\ell=e^{2i\delta_\ell}\) with real phase shift \(\delta_\ell\). The scattered outgoing wave is the change from the unscattered outgoing part, proportional to \(S_\ell-1\).

\[f(\theta)=\frac1k\sum_{\ell=0}^\infty(2\ell+1)e^{i\delta_\ell}\sin\delta_\ell\,P_\ell(\cos\theta),\]
\[\sigma_{\rm sca}=\frac{4\pi}{k^2}\sum_\ell(2\ell+1)\sin^2\delta_\ell .\]

The second result follows by integrating \(|f|^2\) and using \(\int_{-1}^{1}P_\ell P_m\,du=2\delta_{\ell m}/(2\ell+1)\). At forward angle all Legendre functions equal one. The imaginary part of \(e^{i\delta}\sin\delta\) is \(\sin^2\delta\), exactly the quantity appearing in the integrated cross section. This equality in each uncoupled channel proves the elastic optical theorem in the scalar example. It does not require the forward intensity \(|f(0)|^2\) to have any particular value. The calibrated phase of the forward amplitude is essential.

For a hypothetical acoustic sphere at wavenumber \(k=20\) per metre, suppose only the scalar zeroth phase shift is appreciable and has \(\delta_0=\pi/6\). Its scattering amplitude has real part 0.0217 m and imaginary part 0.0125 m, independent of viewing angle. Integrating its differential cross section gives \(4\pi(0.025\,\mathrm m)^2=0.00785\) square metres. The forward identity gives \(4\pi(0.0125)/20=0.00785\) square metres as well. Shifting the phase through \(\pi/2\) at this wavenumber increases the cross section by four, to the single-channel elastic maximum.

What an incomplete outgoing channel tells the experimenter

For an absorbing or internally converting target write \(S_\ell=\eta_\ell e^{2i\delta_\ell}\), \(0\leq\eta_\ell\leq1\). The missing outgoing flux in that exterior channel contributes to absorption or to unobserved channels, depending on the physical system. Define \(a_\ell=(S_\ell-1)/(2i)\), so the scalar wave amplitude above uses \(a_\ell=e^{i\delta_\ell}\sin\delta_\ell\) in the purely elastic limit. Elementary algebra gives

\[\operatorname{Im}a_\ell-|a_\ell|^2=\frac{1-|S_\ell|^2}{4},\]
\[\sigma_{\rm abs}=\frac{\pi}{k^2}\sum_\ell(2\ell+1)(1-\eta_\ell^2).\]

For a single channel with \(\eta=0\), the scattered amplitude is \(i/2\). The residual coherent outgoing component relative to its incident plane-wave background corresponds to a finite scattered power even though this channel has zero total outgoing radial amplitude. Its absorbed and scattered cross sections are equal, while extinction is their sum. This distinction is the angular-channel counterpart of interference between incident and scattered fields in the forward direction.

An experiment may infer \(\eta<1\) because the detector fails to collect another outgoing polarisation, a converted elastic mode or a large-angle part of the pattern. To assign true heat absorption one needs the full exterior channel accounting or a calorimetric measurement. For a homogeneous liquid suspension distinguish energy dissipated in the nominal particles from energy dissipated in the surrounding medium. The preceding cross-section definitions assume a nonabsorbing reference host, so a lossy host requires an explicit incident-reference subtraction and an agreed control surface.

Suppose a measurement models the target only as an isotropic scalar scatterer, yet detects a pronounced forward lobe. A one-channel fit can reproduce a forward complex amplitude by choosing an appropriate complex \(a_0\), but it also predicts the same intensity at every other angle. Add a possible \(\ell=1\) component and measure at \(\theta=0,\pi/2,\pi\). At right angles the term proportional to \(P_1\) vanishes, which helps isolate the \(\ell=0\) component. Sum and difference of complex forward and backward amplitudes separate even and odd parity. Intensity-only data lose signs and relative phase and may require additional incident conditions or interferometry.

To check a numerical partial-wave calculation, increase the highest retained order at fixed geometry and compare the integrated angular quadrature with the coefficient sum. These diagnostics test the angular grid and truncation separately. A narrow forward peak may contribute little at any grid point if no nodes resolve its centre, yet contribute substantially to extinction. Gauss–Legendre quadrature in \(u=\cos\theta\) automatically includes the solid-angle measure. Finally compare the coefficient-sum extinction against the imaginary forward value with the chosen time convention. A sign error in outgoing radial waves can produce a beautifully converged negative extinction for a passive sample.

Track the frequency dependence of a scattering signal

Change the ratio of forcing to resonance frequency. Decide which part of the response determines a scattering measurement and compare it with the power balance in the text.

Open the live experiment

Worked suspension problem · infer and check an optical depth

A dilute suspension has independently measured single-particle extinction cross section \(5.0\times10^{-15}\) square metres and particle density \(3.0\times10^{14}\) per cubic metre. A narrow stop rejects the scattered halo. Predict the direct transmission through 20 mm and estimate how many particles occupy a cylindrical illuminated volume of radius 0.10 mm. Which approximation is most vulnerable if the radius is increased?

Derive the transport law and identify the role of the aperture

In an infinitesimal length \(dz\), the direct-beam intensity loses a fraction \(n\sigma_{\rm ext}dz\) in the independent, uniform, dilute limit. Integrating gives \(I/I_0=e^{-n\sigma_{\rm ext}L}\). The extinction coefficient is 1.50 per metre and optical depth 0.0300, implying direct transmission 0.9704. A first-order calculation gives 0.9700. The small difference is consistent with neglect of higher powers in optical depth, but the exponential by itself does not describe multiple scattering into the receiver.

The illuminated volume is \(\pi(10^{-4})^2(0.020)=6.28\times10^{-10}\) cubic metres and contains about 188,000 particles on average. Increasing beam radius at fixed density and path length raises this count but leaves ideal optical depth unchanged. Counting more independent scatterers reduces relative sample fluctuations. It can, however, change beam divergence, spatial illumination and what scattered angles reach a finite detector. There is no general reason for local diluteness to fail merely because the beam area is larger.

Interchange cells of several lengths and measure the slope of the logarithm of transmitted intensity. An intercept different from zero suggests interface loss or a calibration offset if both incident and transmitted powers are referenced outside the cell. Repeat the length scan with a wider angular stop. A reduced slope with the wide stop signals that the detector collects part of the scattering halo; a genuine absorption contribution persists. At higher densities, evaluate interactions between particles or coherent structure before extrapolating the dilute-solution cross section from the same attenuation law.

A scattering measurement weights the large end of a distribution

The small-particle scattering law grows as the sixth power of radius for fixed material contrast and wavelength. A suspension with a modest spread of radii can therefore scatter substantially more than a calculation using the mean radius for every particle. If radii are independently distributed with mean \(a_0\) and small fractional standard deviation \(s\), expand \(a^6=a_0^6(1+\delta)^6\). The first correction to the ensemble mean is \(15s^2\) times the monodisperse value when the centred deviations average to zero. At ten percent relative spread, the leading increase is fifteen percent.

Separately measuring total particle material changes the comparison. Material volume scales as the third power, whose corresponding correction is \(3s^2\). At fixed material volume, the scattering per unit volume therefore increases by approximately \(12s^2\) relative to spheres of mean radius, within the same narrow-distribution approximation. Reporting the quantity held fixed changes the numerical prediction meaningfully.

A few larger outliers can invalidate this expansion and even take the sample beyond the Rayleigh regime. Measure a size distribution independently and average the single-particle cross section over that distribution, including detector acceptance if it varies with size. A fit to a single apparent radius may match total intensity at one wavelength while failing its spectral slope or angular pattern. Comparing two wavelengths and a separate volume estimate gives a practical check on that hidden weighting.

Tutorial · examine two proposed dipole fits for passivity

At a fixed wavelength two fits to an angular intensity pattern return \(b_1=0.30+0.10i\) and \(b_2=0.30-0.10i\), with \(b=k^3\alpha/(6\pi)\). Can angular intensity distinguish them? Find normalized scattering, extinction and absorption by dividing each cross section by \(6\pi/k^2\). Which fit can describe a passive particle?

Compare interference with scattered intensity

Both have \(|b|^2=0.10\), giving the same angular scattering and normalized scattered cross section 0.10. Extinction in these units is \(\Im b\), hence +0.10 or −0.10. Absorption is the difference, zero or −0.20. The first sits on the lossless passivity circle. The second requires an active energy supply if described by these conventions. Even an angular pattern measured over the whole sphere would retain that ambiguity because it contains squared magnitudes. The forward interference phase or an independent extinction reading discriminates.

Tutorial · recover a particle radius with a stated systematic uncertainty

At fixed wavelength and index a dilute sample scatters 1.50 times the reference signal per unit volume. Assume initially the same number density and a Rayleigh regime. Infer the radius ratio. Then allow number density to be 20% higher than assumed. Estimate how much of the signal difference could instead be a concentration effect, and propose a phase-sensitive check of the dipole interpretation.

Track the powers of radius and concentration separately

Scattered power is proportional to \(Na^6\) under these assumptions, so the radius ratio is \(1.50^{1/6}=1.070\). If number density is actually 1.20 times the reference, the ratio becomes \((1.50/1.20)^{1/6}=1.038\). An apparently precise intensity can therefore constrain a combined quantity quite well without determining either factor separately. Measure an independent particle count or concentration. A forward extinction measurement should satisfy the optical theorem, while the real part of the dilute coherent refractive response scales mainly with \(N\Re\alpha\sim Na^3\). Combining these observables is better conditioned than repeating a single intensity measurement.

Barton Zwiebach, MIT · optical theorem and the forward scattering amplitude

David Tong, University of Cambridge · scattering, induced dipoles and electromagnetic radiation

Diffraction from the near field to the focal plane

Waves, radiation and relativity

An illuminated aperture is a boundary condition for a wave. A screen a millimetre away, an intensity camera at a lens focus and a microscope measuring an evanescent field sample different consequences of the same condition. We will derive operators for propagating the field, estimate the approximations each instrument makes and use energy and sampling checks to tell numerical structure from a measurable fringe.

How each spatial frequency propagates

Take a monochromatic scalar field with physical value given by the real part of \(U(x,y,z)e^{-i\omega t}\). This approximates one transverse component when the polarisation remains nearly uniform and angles are small. In a homogeneous transparent half-space it obeys the Helmholtz equation. Specify the complex amplitude in the plane \(z=0\) and require outgoing or decaying waves for positive \(z\). Fourier transform the two transverse coordinates, leaving a differential equation in the third.

\[\partial_z^2\widehat U+(k^2-k_x^2-k_y^2)\widehat U=0,\]
\[\widehat U(k_x,k_y,z)=\widehat U(k_x,k_y,0)e^{ik_z z},\]
\[k_z=\sqrt{k^2-k_x^2-k_y^2}.\]

Choose the root with positive real part for propagation and positive imaginary part for decay. Each propagating component advances in phase. Hence power integrated over a plane is unchanged for a paraxial beam with no absorbing aperture downstream. For angles approaching grazing incidence the weight for normal flux contains the direction cosine. Components with transverse wavenumber exceeding \(k\) decay exponentially. An aperture map thus contains structures invisible to a distant measurement even if the map itself is known exactly.

In vacuum a sinusoidal field structure of period 400 nm illuminated at 600 nm has \(k_x/k=1.50\). Its amplitude has fallen to \(1/e\) after \(1/(k\sqrt{1.5^2-1})=85.4\) nm. At 500 nm the remaining amplitude is 0.00287 and the squared amplitude is roughly eight parts in a million of its plane value. A numerical back-propagation that reinstates the original component also multiplies measurement errors by about 350. Improving detector gain does not restore the unknown phase.

A propagation kernel with a controlled phase error

For small transverse wavenumber, expand \(k_z=k-k_\perp^2/(2k)-k_\perp^4/(8k^3)+\cdots\). The first correction becomes a chirp in Fourier space. Completing the square in its inverse transform gives the Fresnel kernel. Write \(\mathbf r\) for a transverse observation point and \(\boldsymbol\xi\) for a point in the aperture.

\[U(\mathbf r,z)\simeq\frac{e^{ikz}}{i\lambda z}\int U(\boldsymbol\xi,0)\exp\!\left[\frac{ik}{2z}|\mathbf r-\boldsymbol\xi|^2\right]d^2\xi .\]

This expression retains phase curvature across the aperture. The coefficient ensures that a spatially uniform boundary amplitude reproduces a uniform plane wave. An uncovered infinite plane is a useful test for a numerical convolution, although a finite computation needs padding or a truly periodic uniform test. A measured slit has sharp edges with broad spatial spectra, so peak error near an edge can exceed the error suggested by a small angle evaluated for the central lobe.

At an observation angle \(\theta\) a propagation distance \(z\) accumulates neglected phase of order \(kz\sin^4\theta/8\). For \(z=0.10\) m, \(\lambda=500\) nm and \(\theta=0.01\) rad this is 0.00157 rad. At 0.05 rad it is close to one radian. A faint sidelobe at a moderately large angle can require exact angular-spectrum propagation even while a paraxial estimate describes the central spot beautifully.

Which quadratic term can the far field lose?

Expand the kernel's square into an observation phase, a source phase and a bilinear term. Only the source phase can be dropped without changing the predicted intensity directly through that factor. For source radius \(a\) its maximum value is \(\pi a^2/(\lambda z)\). The remaining integral is the Fourier transform of the aperture at spatial frequencies \(x/(\lambda z),y/(\lambda z)\). Define the Fresnel number using the half-width \(a\). A convention based on full slit width gives a value four times larger.

\[U(\mathbf r,z)\simeq\frac{e^{ikz+ikr^2/(2z)}}{i\lambda z}\,\widehat U\!\left(\frac{kx}{z},\frac{ky}{z},0\right),\]
\[N_F=\frac{a^2}{\lambda z}.\]

For a uniformly illuminated slit of full width \(w=0.20\) mm at 532 nm and \(z=1.0\) m, \(N_F=0.0188\). Direct integration of the linear aperture phase gives \(I/I_0=\mathrm{sinc}^2(\pi wx/\lambda z)\), where \(\mathrm{sinc}(u)=\sin(u)/u\). The first zeros should be 2.66 mm either side of centre. The edge phase is 0.059 rad. Moving the screen to 30 mm gives \(N_F=0.627\) and edge phase 1.97 rad. Substituting the smaller distance in the far-field formula yields a number but no longer a controlled approximation.

Compare near- and far-field predictions in the slit integral

In that experiment reduce the mesh interval before moving the screen. If the exposed variable counts panels, doubling panels at fixed aperture halves the interval. A converged difference between the full and far-field integrals is a physical failure of the approximation. A feature that moves each time the number of source samples doubles is a numerical failure. Keep the same physical horizontal axis and the same normalisation when comparing adjacent screens.

From a circular pupil to a resolution measurement

When the pupil is a disk, polar coordinates considerably simplify the Fourier integral. Align the transverse transform vector along the horizontal axis. The angular average of \(e^{-iqr\cos\phi}\) defines \(J_0(qr)\). One radial integration, using \(d[uJ_1(u)]/du=uJ_0(u)\), then gives the result. Divide by the on-axis amplitude, proportional to aperture area.

\[\frac{U(q)}{U(0)}=\frac{2}{a^2}\int_0^a rJ_0(qr)\,dr=\frac{2J_1(qa)}{qa},\]
\[q=k\sin\theta .\]

At \(qa=0\) use \(J_1(s)\sim s/2\). The first zero is at \(qa=3.8317\). Hence a disk of diameter \(D\) has its first dark ring at \(\sin\theta=1.22\lambda/D\). Two mutually incoherent identical points contribute the sum of their intensities. Rayleigh's conventional separation places each peak on the first zero of its neighbour. A coherent pair instead has an interference term that depends on the unknown relative phase. The same separation criterion cannot predict its measured trough without that phase.

A 5.0 mm pupil on a 100 mm focal-length lens at 550 nm gives a first-zero radius of 13.4 micrometres on the focal plane. A camera with 6.5 micrometre pixels spans the full bright core with about four pixels. Fitting the central pixel alone to find the image position would depend strongly on whether the spot lay on a pixel centre or a corner. A joint fit to integrated pixel intensities can estimate a position more precisely than the image width when enough photons are detected. That precision describes one isolated spot and does not directly give a two-source resolution.

Why a lens puts the spectrum close at hand

A thin positive lens adds the quadratic phase \(-kr^2/(2f)\) to a field immediately in front of it. At the plane \(z=f\), this cancels the source term of the Fresnel kernel exactly within paraxial theory. The focal intensity therefore samples a Fourier spectrum even for a tabletop lens standing much nearer than the free-space far-field distance. The physical exit pupil remains part of the amplitude. Closing it removes high transverse spatial frequencies in an imaging arrangement and broadens the spot of a point emitter.

For illumination with Gaussian amplitude \(U\propto e^{-r^2/w^2}\), completing the transverse Fourier integrals gives another Gaussian, with focal-plane amplitude radius \(w_0=\lambda f/(\pi w)\) for a broad lens and an incident flat wavefront. If \(w=1.0\) mm, \(f=100\) mm and \(\lambda=633\) nm, \(w_0=20.1\) micrometres. This is the \(1/e\) field radius, also the \(1/e^2\) intensity radius. The Rayleigh length \(\pi w_0^2/\lambda\) is 2.02 mm. Moving a detector one Rayleigh length off the waist increases the radius by \(\sqrt2\) and halves its on-axis intensity for fixed total beam power.

Use the ray-bundle experiment to locate the geometric focus before estimating its diffractive width

From a point image to transmitted object contrast

A microscope may resolve individual test objects yet deliver very little contrast for a periodic specimen at a similar length scale. The reason is that incoherent imaging acts linearly on intensity, while coherent imaging acts linearly on field amplitude. Start with mutually incoherent object points. Their expected image intensities add, so the image is the convolution of object intensity with a nonnegative intensity point-spread function \(h=|h_A|^2\), where \(h_A\) is the coherent amplitude impulse response. The Fourier transform of \(h\), normalised to one at zero spatial frequency, is the optical transfer function. Its magnitude describes attenuation of a sinusoidal intensity modulation.

For a focused unaberrated system the coherent amplitude response in Fourier space is the pupil function. The convolution theorem applied to \(|h_A|^2\) shows that the incoherent transfer function is the pupil autocorrelation. For a clear circular pupil this amounts to asking how much two equal disks overlap as their centres separate. The overlap consists of two circular sectors minus their triangular parts. With normalised frequency \(s=f/f_c\) and incoherent cutoff \(f_c=2\,\mathrm{NA}/\lambda_0\) in object space, the result is

\[M(s)=\frac2\pi[\arccos s-s\sqrt{1-s^2}],\]
\[0\le s\le1,\]
\[M(s)=0\quad(s>1).\]

Here \(\lambda_0\) is vacuum wavelength and \(\mathrm{NA}=n\sin\theta_{\max}\). At \(s=0\) the disks coincide and the full mean intensity passes. At the cutoff their edges just touch and sinusoidal contrast vanishes. The coherent pupil transmits field spatial frequencies only through \(\mathrm{NA}/\lambda_0\). These two cutoffs answer different questions about different object and illumination statistics. A coherent phase grating viewed in exact focus can yield an almost uniform intensity image despite transmitting several field components.

For \(\mathrm{NA}=0.25\) at 550 nm, the incoherent cutoff is 909 cycles/mm. A sinusoidal target of 450 cycles/mm has \(s=0.495\) and \(M\simeq0.396\). An object with 0.60 sinusoidal intensity visibility is predicted to show about 0.238 before detector averaging. If the object-referred pixel width is 0.75 micrometres, rectangular pixel integration multiplies this contrast by \(\mathrm{sinc}(\pi fp)=0.823\); the recorded visibility is about 0.196. Nyquist frequency for this pixel spacing is 667 cycles/mm. Hence even some frequencies that the optics can transmit lie above the detector Nyquist limit and can alias back to lower apparent frequencies.

This prediction can be tested with an adjustable magnification while keeping entrance pupil and object fixed. Object-referred pixel width falls as magnification rises; a pixel-limited measurement should approach the optical transfer curve. Raising magnification also spreads light over more pixels, changing per-pixel count noise, so compare fitted contrasts with uncertainty. At frequencies where the ideal transfer is small, a high-contrast target and low read noise matter. Dividing by an arbitrarily small transfer function to sharpen the final image can turn noise into a convincing periodic pattern.

A pupil-phase error redistributes energy without absorbing it

Introduce a known defocus into a clear circular pupil. Let the phase relative to its centre vary as \(\phi(r)=\Phi r^2/a^2\). The on-axis focal amplitude is proportional to the pupil average of \(e^{i\phi}\), since all ideal contributions arrive in phase there. Integrate in polar coordinates and substitute \(u=r^2/a^2\). Relative to the perfect on-axis intensity the result is

\[S=\left|\int_0^1e^{i\Phi u}du\right|^2=\left[\frac{\sin(\Phi/2)}{\Phi/2}\right]^2 .\]

This special-case Strehl ratio assumes a uniform pupil and pure quadratic defocus. Removing a phase piston makes no change to intensity. Expanding for small \(\Phi\) yields \(S=1-\Phi^2/12+\cdots\). The pupil-phase variance after removing the mean piston is \(\Phi^2/12\), which explains the leading connection between reduced peak intensity and wavefront variance without assuming that every type of aberration has the same pattern.

A quarter of a wave of centre-to-edge optical path error gives \(\Phi=\pi/2\) and \(S=0.811\). One half wave gives 0.405. In an ideal phase-only pupil the missing on-axis intensity appears elsewhere in the image; total transmitted power over a sufficiently wide observation plane is unchanged. Contrast this with an absorbing pupil that removes a uniform fraction of intensity everywhere. Measuring only the brightest pixel confuses the two. Measure integrated power over increasing radii and fit a spatial profile as well as a peak.

To turn this into an axial calibration, derive the edge phase from the distance mismatch. For a paraxial marginal ray in air making angle approximately \(\mathrm{NA}\), an axial shift \(\Delta z\) introduces a relative phase magnitude near \(k\,|\Delta z|\,\mathrm{NA}^2/2\). At \(\mathrm{NA}=0.10\), \(\lambda=500\) nm and \(|\Delta z|=25\) micrometres this is 1.57 rad, the quarter-wave case. With a larger numerical aperture use actual longitudinal wavevectors instead of the quadratic approximation and model polarisation where required. The formulas predict the same on-axis drop for positive and negative defocus. A single peak reading cannot give the sign of an axial misplacement; scanning through focus supplies that missing sign and can reveal an asymmetric aberration.

Distinguish aperture physics from quadrature error

Refine the aperture samples and vary their physical extent independently. A moving numerical null and a true diffraction minimum have different refinement signatures.

Open the live experiment

Worked optical problem · determine how much light fits into one Airy disk

Two groups compare image quality using respectively the peak intensity and the energy contained inside the first dark ring. Derive the encircled-energy fraction for a uniform circular pupil and obtain its value at the first ring. Would closing the aperture increase the useful energy fraction on a detector of fixed physical radius?

Integrate the radial intensity with its area measure

Write \(v=ka\sin\theta\), and use the small-angle focal-plane variable proportional to image radius. Focal intensity varies as \([2J_1(v)/v]^2\). The annular area element supplies a factor \(v\,dv\). Use \(J'_0=-J_1\) and \(J'_1=J_0-J_1/v\) to differentiate \(J_0^2+J_1^2\). The derivative is \(-2J_1^2/v\), giving the fraction inside radius \(v\) as \(1-J_0(v)^2-J_1(v)^2\). It tends to zero at the origin and to one at very large radius, which fixes the integration constants and normalisation without needing an absolute incident amplitude.

At \(v=3.8317\), \(J_1=0\) and \(J_0\simeq-0.4028\). The encircled fraction is therefore 0.838. The remaining 16.2 percent is outside the central disk even in a perfect ideal image. If the physical detector radius corresponded to this value before closing the pupil, reducing the pupil radius decreases \(v\) at the detector edge and therefore decreases the collected fraction. It also ordinarily reduces the total incident power transmitted by the pupil.

Peak-normalised plots rescale each pupil's image to one at its centre, which hides both effects. For a fair power comparison record the total incident flux, the pupil transmission and the energy on the fixed detector. A phase aberration or obscuration can leave the nominal first-zero position a poor boundary for comparing different pupils. The same physical encircled radius offers a reproducible metric in those cases, provided the beam centre and background are estimated independently.

A phase ramp moves a diffraction pattern without changing its shape

Illuminate a thin aperture with a tilted plane wave in the paraxial regime. At the aperture plane this multiplies its complex amplitude by a linear phase ramp. The Fourier shift theorem then moves the entire focal-plane amplitude by a transverse amount \(f\sin\theta_i\) along the tilt direction. Its intensity pattern retains its shape as long as the illumination envelope fills the pupil uniformly and the lens has enough acceptance. Tilting a narrow 532 nm beam by one milliradian in front of a 100 mm lens moves the pattern by about one tenth of a millimetre.

This provides a practical separation of phase tilt from aperture narrowing. A tilt moves both first zeros in the same direction, whereas changing the width of a centred slit moves the zeros apart or together. A position fit using one zero alone cannot separate these perturbations. Jointly fitting both zeros yields a mean position sensitive to tilt and a separation sensitive to width. A scanned detector with unequal position calibration on the two sides can mix the two observables.

For a spatially incoherent angular source, integrate intensities of the shifted patterns over the angular source distribution. An extended source then blurs zeros that would be sharp under one coherent angle. A coherent superposition of the same angular components instead requires adding the shifted amplitudes and can produce additional fringes. The illumination statistics determine which calculation describes the recorded image.

Tutorial · Distinguish a pupil defect from pixel integration

In a slit focal pattern the central lobe has one-sided first-zero distance 40 micrometres. An analyst samples precisely at that first zero using a centred pixel 8 micrometres wide and finds positive intensity. Estimate the finite-pixel fraction and propose a measurement that would expose an additional scattered background.

Show the pixel calculation and a discriminating test

Let \(x=x_0+s\), where \(|s|\) is much smaller than \(x_0\). The sinc amplitude near its zero is approximately \(-s/x_0\), giving intensity \(I/I_\mathrm{peak}\simeq s^2/x_0^2\). Averaging across a pixel of width \(p\) gives \(p^2/(12x_0^2)\), about \(3.33\times10^{-3}\). This estimate uses a flat response within each pixel and a sample centred on the true minimum. A two-micrometre centring error contributes about another 0.0025 at leading order.

Use magnification that doubles the first-zero distance in pixel units while keeping pupil and wavelength fixed. Pixel leakage at a centred zero should then fall by four relative to the peak. A background proportional to local scattered light may respond differently and a dark offset can be estimated from opaque-frame counts. Calibrate differing exposure and throughput, subtract dark counts prior to normalisation and fit both flanks to locate the true minimum.

Tutorial · Design a propagation-grid check

A 512-point transverse grid with 2 micrometre spacing represents an aperture at 600 nm. What angle can the grid represent? Could its periodically repeated boundary contaminate a nominal 0.08 rad bundle after 10 mm propagation, if the beam spreads from the centre? Explain two refinements that test distinct errors.

Show the angular and spatial budgets

Nyquist gives \(|k_x|\leq\pi/\Delta x\), hence \(|\sin\theta|\leq\lambda/(2\Delta x)=0.15\). The 0.08 rad direction is resolved in principle, although modes near Nyquist would need a separate resolution study. Its lateral displacement over 10 mm is about 0.802 mm, larger than the 0.512 mm half-window even for a beam initially confined to its centre. A periodic FFT can therefore wrap light around into the opposite side.

Double the number of points with spacing fixed. This doubles the window while keeping the represented angular limit fixed. Then halve the spacing while doubling the number again to leave this enlarged window fixed and test transverse discretisation independently. Compare conserved flux, central intensity and the location of the first minimum, retaining absolute normalisation. Flux conservation in a periodic window can hold perfectly even when light has wrapped across its boundary. A plot of that single diagnostic would miss the contamination.

MIT 2.71 Optics · Fourier optics, apertures and propagation

Measure the phase speed of a numerical electromagnetic wave

Numerical field laboratory

Start with a lossless Maxwell wave, build a second-order time update and compare its actual travel time with what the continuum wave equation predicts.

A pulse can arrive late without losing energy

In a uniform lossless one-dimensional material take electric field along \(y\), magnetic field along \(z\) and propagation along \(x\). Maxwell's curl laws reduce to \(\epsilon E_t=-H_x\) and \(\mu H_t=-E_x\). Differentiate the first in time and substitute the second to obtain \(E_{tt}=c^2 E_{xx}\), \(c=1/\sqrt{\mu\epsilon}\). A real laboratory signal is a combination of spatial frequencies. Different errors in travel time for different components can distort its shape even when the scheme does not attenuate any resolved Fourier mode.

Use periodic boundaries so that reflection from an end cannot be confused with this effect. Scale distance by one wavelength and time by one exact period. The periodic field \(E(x,t)=\sin[2\pi(x-t)]\) has unit amplitude and returns to its initial shape after every integer period. A probe comparing just its envelope would miss a discrepancy in phase.

Derive the discrete dispersion relation

Take points separated by \(h\) and times separated by \(\Delta t\). Centred second differences give \(u_j^{n+1}=2u_j^n-u_j^{n-1}+C^2(u_{j+1}^n-2u_j^n+u_{j-1}^n)\), with \(C=c\Delta t/h\). Insert the complex trial field \(e^{i(j\theta-n\Omega)}\). The time pair contributes \(2\cos\Omega-2\), the spatial pair \(2\cos\theta-2\). The physical positive-frequency branch obeys

\[\sin(\Omega/2)=C\sin(\theta/2),\]
\[\frac{c_\mathrm{phase,grid}}{c}=\frac{\Omega}{C\theta}.\]

If \(0<C\le1\) a real \(\Omega\) exists for every grid phase, and each Fourier mode has constant discrete amplitude in exact arithmetic when started on one travelling branch. For \(C>1\) sufficiently rapid spatial oscillations have no real frequency. One time factor then grows. Testing stability on a single very smooth wave can miss this unstable part of the supported spectrum.

For long waves expand \(\sin\) and \(\arcsin\): \(\Omega=C\theta+C(C^2-1)\theta^3/24+O(\theta^5)\). The relative phase-speed error is \((C^2-1)\theta^2/24\) at leading order. Here increasing \(C\) towards one improves wave propagation. This special cancellation depends on a uniform one-dimensional grid, constant wave speed and the selected difference operators.

Initialise a clean numerical experiment

A second-order recurrence needs two time levels. Taking \(u_j^0=\sin(j\theta)\) and \(u_j^{-1}=\sin(j\theta+\Omega)\) excites a single right-going numerical mode. Using the exact continuum phase \(C\theta\) at the second level instead introduces a small mixture of right- and left-travelling grid modes. That is a valid test of initialisation, but it mixes errors if the quantity of interest is solely the numerical dispersion relation. State in your notes how the second level was produced.

Open the live experiment

The browser actually advances the nodal difference equations. It then reports the dispersion prediction as an independent check. The time is chosen as an integer number of numerical steps close to five exact periods and is stated with the result. At twelve points per wavelength and \(C=0.8\) there are fifteen steps per period. Both exact and discrete solutions have the same amplitude to roundoff. At the end of five periods examine how far their zero crossings have separated.

From a phase test to a pulse test

For a narrow packet, envelope motion is set by group velocity \(d\omega/dk\). Differentiate the discrete relation at fixed \(h,\Delta t\). The result is \(v_g/c=\cos(\theta/2)/\sqrt{1-C^2\sin^2(\theta/2)}\) on the positive branch for resolved nonendpoint modes. At \(C=0.8\) a mode close to the grid Nyquist limit has small group speed. A wave carrier and its envelope can show different speed errors. In a real dielectric wavelength dependence may also come from the material itself; a grid-refinement test with unchanged material law separates these contributions.

As an independent benchmark, set \(C=1\). Then \(\Omega=\theta\) for \(0\le\theta\le\pi\), and the right-going recurrence translates the samples exactly by one grid cell at each time step. The lab should align both curves even on a surprisingly coarse grid for this particular smooth periodic mode. This checks the sign and indexing of propagation without implying exact accuracy for arbitrary geometry and material interfaces.

Tutorial

A signal must accumulate less than 1 degree of phase error over ten periods. Estimate a required points-per-wavelength count for \(C=0.5\) using the leading error.

Work through the solution

The total continuum phase advance is \(20\pi\) rad. Requiring \(20\pi(1-C^2)(2\pi/N)^2/24<\pi/180\) gives \(N^2>450\pi^2\), or \(N\gtrsim67\). Evaluate the exact dispersion relation to check the chosen integer.

Why is measuring only the field at its crest a poor detector of small phase lag?

Work through the solution

The derivative of a sinusoid vanishes at its crest. A small phase shift changes that value only quadratically; at a zero crossing the change is linear. A phase fit using the entire ring is more robust than either single sample.

The numerical envelope changes after a wall is introduced. What additional tests would separate dispersion from reflection errors?

Work through the solution

First validate travel time on a uniform periodic domain. Next use a single known interface with an analytical reflection coefficient. Move the observation plane and refine the mesh separately. Compare reflected phase and amplitude with an energy-flux balance so a boundary error cannot masquerade as bulk dispersion.

Separate diffraction from a finite observation window

Numerical field laboratory

Recover the far-field angular spectrum of a smooth aperture, then isolate the errors from truncating and sampling the aperture field.

Begin with a transform that can be checked analytically

Consider a long slit system whose transmitted scalar field varies along one transverse coordinate \(x\). At a distant observation point in direction \(\theta\), a small aperture element accumulates an approximate relative phase \(-k x\sin\theta\). The Fraunhofer amplitude is proportional to \(\int A(x)e^{-2\pi i f x}\,dx\) with spatial frequency \(f=\sin\theta/\lambda\). The approximation neglects the quadratic phase across the aperture; either a suitable lens or sufficient propagation distance is needed. A numerical Fourier transform cannot cure violation of this optical assumption.

Take the ideal aperture field \(A(x)=e^{-x^2/w^2}\) and normalise far-field amplitude by its on-axis value. Integrating by parts or completing the square gives \(F(f)/F(0)=e^{-(\pi wf)^2}\). One derivation avoids any complex contour: differentiate \(F\) with respect to \(f\) and replace \(xA=-(w^2/2)A'\). Integrating by parts gives \(F'=-2\pi^2 w^2 f F\). Solve this first-order equation with the measured \(F(0)\). Thus a wider beam on the aperture narrows the far-field distribution. Squaring the amplitude gives the intensity measured by an ordinary detector.

At \(w=1\) mm, the \(1/e\) amplitude spatial frequency is \(1/(\pi w)\simeq0.3183\) cycles/mm. For 500 nm light this is a small angle of \(1.59\times10^{-4}\) radians. An instrument calibrated in radians per millimetre rather than cycles per millimetre would need a factor \(2\pi\); stating the Fourier convention prevents that frequent scale discrepancy.

What the instrument has really sampled

A scanner covers \(-W\le x\le W\) and gives one amplitude at each of \(N\) equally spaced cell midpoints. The numerical transform is the weighted sum \(\Delta x\sum_j A(x_j)e^{-2\pi ifx_j}\), where \(\Delta x=2W/N\). By symmetry its imaginary part vanishes for a perfectly centred real Gaussian; the program sums the cosine projection. It normalises by the numerical on-axis amplitude, allowing us to compare shapes on a common vertical scale.

Two separate approximations have entered. Truncating the aperture multiplies the field by a rectangle. In frequency space this convolves the Gaussian spectrum with a sinc and can produce signed sidelobes. Sampling at regular intervals makes the spectrum repeat up to a phase convention, with alias copies separated by \(1/\Delta x\). Increasing \(W\) at fixed \(N\) reduces truncation but worsens sampling. Widening the field of view and adding more detector pixels are not interchangeable changes.

Design a convergence test with two axes

Open the live experiment

Keep \(w=1\) mm and select 40 samples. Compare \(W=w\) and \(W=3w\). The aperture field at the boundary falls from \(e^{-1}\) to \(e^{-9}\), so an apparent sidelobe associated with the abrupt window should decrease strongly. Next fix \(W=3w\) and lower the number of samples. The displayed Nyquist frequency falls as \(\Delta x\) grows. When a plotted point lies above it, a smooth-looking value is an aliased sample of the periodic discrete spectrum.

At \(W=2w\), a centred Gaussian retains the on-axis amplitude fraction \(\operatorname{erf}(2)\simeq0.9953\) if integrated exactly. The normalised plot hides this 0.47% loss at zero frequency. If absolute power calibration matters, repeat the experiment without normalisation and independently integrate \(|A|^2\) across the aperture. A shape comparison and a throughput comparison are different measurements, both meaningful.

Recover a size from a far-field measurement

Suppose a detector measures the intensity falling to one half at \(f_{1/2}\). Since \(I/I_0=e^{-2(\pi wf)^2}\), the inferred \(w\) is \(\sqrt{\ln2/2}/(\pi f_{1/2})\). For \(f_{1/2}=0.1874\) cycles/mm the radius is about 1 mm. If the instrument actually reported amplitude half-width the corresponding formula would have \(\sqrt{\ln2}\) in the numerator; the radius would differ by a factor \(\sqrt2\). The raw measured quantity and its threshold should accompany any quoted fitted radius.

A positive intensity cannot distinguish a negative sidelobe amplitude from a positive amplitude of the same magnitude. To observe the sign change shown by the finite-window amplitude curve, interfere the field with a phase reference. Conversely, when comparing with a camera image, square the signed numerical amplitude once and apply the detector pixel average. Comparing a real-amplitude plot directly with a recorded intensity array is an avoidable model mismatch.

Tutorial

How could one distinguish an intrinsic physical sidelobe from ringing produced by the scanner window without a known reference aperture?

Work through the solution

Increase the scanned width while keeping \(\Delta x\) fixed by adding samples. Window artefacts should change as the omitted tails shrink. Repeat with a different smooth weighting and report its effect on resolution. An intrinsic feature that survives these tests can then be compared against an independent physical model.

At \(W=3\) mm and \(N=24\), what spatial frequencies can be represented without aliasing, and what does adding zero padding do?

Work through the solution

\(\Delta x=0.25\) mm, so the Nyquist limit is 2 cycles/mm. Zero padding supplies more displayed frequency samples of the same discrete transform. It adds no measured aperture detail and cannot resolve aliasing already present in the original samples.

A beam shifted by \(x_0\) within a sufficiently wide window has the same far-field intensity. What information changes?

Work through the solution

Substituting \(x'=x-x_0\) in the Fourier integral multiplies its amplitude by \(e^{-2\pi i f x_0}\). The phase ramp records the displacement; the intensity is invariant. A truncated window may break this invariance by clipping different amounts of field.

Estimate a cavity linewidth from a finite, biased record

Numerical field laboratory

Connect the time and frequency descriptions of one damped mode, then diagnose why a weak detector background systematically biases the inferred quality factor.

Know whether the detector reads field or energy

After a drive switches off, let a single cavity mode have complex analytic amplitude \(a(t)=a_0e^{-\kappa t/2}e^{-i\omega_0t}\) for \(t\ge0\). The energy is proportional to \(|a|^2\), giving \(U=U_0e^{-\kappa t}\). Its instantaneous decay rate \(-U'/U=\kappa\) implies \(Q=\omega_0 U/P_\mathrm{loss}=\omega_0/\kappa\). A phase-sensitive receiver measuring field amplitude sees a time constant twice as long as the energy lifetime. Before fitting the data, ask how the measurement transducer converts the field into a reported number.

Fourier transforming the causal decaying amplitude gives an integral proportional to \(1/[\kappa/2-i(\omega-\omega_0)]\), up to the time convention. Its squared magnitude has half-power points at \(|\omega-\omega_0|=\kappa/2\). Therefore the full power linewidth in hertz is \(\Delta f=\kappa/(2\pi)\). A measurement of either the energy decay time \(\tau_U=1/\kappa\) or the spectral width can test the other without having to calibrate the absolute detector gain.

At \(f_0=2.0\) GHz and \(\tau_U=0.50\ \mu\mathrm s\), we obtain \(\kappa=2.0\times10^6\) s⁻¹, \(\Delta f\simeq318.3\) kHz and \(Q\simeq6283\). A field receiver would report an amplitude time constant of 1.0 μs. Substituting that time directly into \(Q=2\pi f_0\tau_U\) would double the apparent quality.

Derive the two-point fit with a detector offset

Let a power detector report \(S(t)=A e^{-\kappa t}+B\), with a constant background \(B>0\). In the absence of background two samples separated by \(\Delta t\) give \(\widehat\kappa=\log[S(t)/S(t+\Delta t)]/\Delta t\). With background, the logarithmic derivative is

\[-\frac{d}{dt}\log S=\kappa\frac{Ae^{-\kappa t}}{Ae^{-\kappa t}+B}.\]

It starts below the true rate and tends to zero in the tail. A straight line fitted to late log data can therefore look impressively precise while overestimating the lifetime. Increasing the acquisition duration can worsen this bias if the entire record is forced into a straight-line model.

In the experiment set \(b=B/A=0.04\). A two-point fit from one true lifetime to two has dimensionless rate \(\log[(e^{-1}+0.04)/(e^{-2}+0.04)]\), approximately 0.844. Its apparent lifetime is about 18.5% high. The source, walls and coupling have not changed. Only the interpretation of detector output has changed. Move the start of the fit towards three lifetimes and inspect the increasing gap from the ideal log line.

Open the live experiment

Design a second independent inference

With three equally spaced, noiseless readings \(S_0,S_1,S_2\) of one exponential plus a constant, subtract consecutive values before taking a logarithm. If \(a=e^{-\kappa\Delta t}\), the differences are \(A'(1-a)\) and \(A'a(1-a)\), giving \(a=(S_1-S_2)/(S_0-S_1)\). This cancels the constant background. At an interval of one lifetime, a first signal of unit energy and background 0.04 gives approximately \(1.04,0.407879,0.175335\). Their ratio of differences recovers \(e^{-1}\). In a real noise floor two nearly equal readings make this division unreliable. Use multiple windows or fit the raw signal with a measured noise model.

Finite spectral resolution creates a separate challenge. Multiplying the measured field by a rectangular window of duration \(T\) convolves its spectrum with a sinc-like response of width of order \(1/T\). For the 318 kHz linewidth in the example, \(T=1\) μs gives frequency bins spaced by 1 MHz in a basic Fourier transform. Zero padding can interpolate its curve, but cannot restore the unmeasured late-time signal. A parametric fit may extract a sub-bin frequency or decay rate by using its model assumptions; its uncertainty then depends on signal-to-noise ratio and on whether the one-mode theory is adequate.

Check the single-mode model before refining its fit

If two nearby modes reach the same detector, their fields add before squaring. The result contains an interference term oscillating at their frequency difference on top of a decaying envelope. A scalar positive background cannot explain that alternating residual. Test by narrowing the excitation band, moving the pickup to a symmetry node of one mode, or examining a complex receiver's quadratures. A model with two exponentials has more degrees of freedom and can fit noise as well as signal; require consistency across independent records.

An honest linewidth report specifies the measured quantity, acquisition window, background treatment, coupling state, reference frequency and uncertainty. A fit to stored energy measures total loss. Inferring internal absorption alone additionally requires a known port coupling or a measurement that changes it separately, as in the course's cavity project.

Tutorial

A detector background is independently measured but subtracted too aggressively. What feature can appear in the logarithmic tail?

Work through the solution

The corrected signal can approach zero and cross below it. The logarithm then becomes undefined. Even before that point its apparent local slope can become arbitrarily large. Inspect the raw signed residual and its uncertainty instead of dropping nonpositive points without accounting for selection bias.

A second experiment doubles the peak stored energy while the absolute detector offset is fixed. What should happen to the shape of the normalised log tail?

Work through the solution

\(b=B/A\) halves. Background contamination becomes important later by about \(\tau_U\ln2\). A real change in the physical lifetime need not follow that shift when the response remains linear.

Can a passive resonator's intrinsic losses be determined from a single ring-down measured at one port?

Work through the solution

The observed decay is the sum of internal and external rates. A single accurately measured lifetime identifies their sum. A calibrated change in coupling or an independent power budget is needed to separate them.

Reconstructing a partially polarised electromagnetic wave

Electrodynamics

A detector averages enormous numbers of optical cycles. Its horizontal and vertical readings may be equal for a circularly polarised wave, a diagonal linearly polarised wave, or unpolarised light. Which additional intensities distinguish these preparations?

Start from what a polarising analyser measures

Take a quasi-monochromatic beam propagating in a fixed direction, resolved in transverse axes \(x,y\). Use complex field amplitudes with time factor \(e^{-i\omega t}\). Absorb common power and impedance factors into their units. The time- or ensemble-averaged coherency matrix \(J=\langle\boldsymbol E\boldsymbol E^\dagger\rangle\) has four real degrees of freedom because it is Hermitian. An analyser selecting the normalised complex state \(\boldsymbol a\) passes amplitude \(\boldsymbol a^\dagger\boldsymbol E\) and measures \(I_a=\boldsymbol a^\dagger J\boldsymbol a\). Thus every analyser projects the same matrix onto a distinct polarisation.

Choose \(S_0=J_{xx}+J_{yy}\), \(S_1=J_{xx}-J_{yy}\), \(S_2=2\operatorname{Re}J_{xy}\), and \(S_3=-2\operatorname{Im}J_{xy}\). For this convention the state \((1,i)/\sqrt2\) has positive \(S_3\). Naming it by its explicit Jones vector avoids a handedness ambiguity between observer viewing directions. Inverting those definitions gives

\[J=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}S_0+S_1&S_2-iS_3\\S_2+iS_3&S_0-S_1\end{pmatrix}.\]

Horizontal and vertical linear analysers therefore give \((S_0\pm S_1)/2\). The \(+45^\circ,-45^\circ\) pair gives \((S_0\pm S_2)/2\). The circular pair \((1,\pm i)/\sqrt2\) gives \((S_0\pm S_3)/2\). Every pair sum recovers total intensity, providing an internal calibration check. Subtracting the complementary measurements cancels a common additive detector offset in each Stokes difference, though \(S_0\) still needs a dark correction.

Derive the physically allowed domain

Because \(I_a\ge0\) for every analyser, both eigenvalues of \(J\) are nonnegative. Solve its quadratic characteristic equation using the trace \(S_0\) and determinant \((S_0^2-S_1^2-S_2^2-S_3^2)/4\). The eigenvalues are \((S_0\pm|\boldsymbol S|)/2\), where \(\boldsymbol S=(S_1,S_2,S_3)\). Consequently \(P=|\boldsymbol S|/S_0\) lies between zero and one for a beam with nonzero intensity. A single fully polarised Jones state gives rank one and \(P=1\). An incoherent mixture of two orthogonal states of equal intensity has \(J=(S_0/2)I\) and \(P=0\).

Suppose a corrected data set gives \(I_x,I_y=70,30\); \(I_{+45},I_{-45}=65,35\); \(I_+,I_-=60,40\) in the same units. The pair sums agree at 100. Reconstructed parameters are \((100,40,30,20)\), giving \(P=\sqrt{2900}/100=0.539\). The eigenvalues are 76.93 and 23.07. Subtracting the smaller times the identity leaves a rank-one contribution of intensity 53.85 and an unpolarised remainder of intensity 46.15. This decomposition describes the observed second moments; several microscopic preparations can produce the same \(J\).

Predict a rotating analyser and identify its blind spot

For \(\boldsymbol a=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha)\), expanding the projection and using double-angle identities gives \(I_\alpha=[S_0+S_1\cos2\alpha+S_2\sin2\alpha]/2\). A linear analyser alone is blind to \(S_3\), even when sampled at hundreds of angles. In the example the extrema are \(50\pm25\), with the maximum at \(\alpha=\frac12\operatorname{atan2}(30,40)=18.43^\circ\). The linear fringe visibility 0.50 is slightly below the total degree 0.539. Inserting a quarter-wave plate with Jones matrix \(\operatorname{diag}(1,-i)\) before the same analyser transforms \(S_2\) into \(S_3\) in the intensity formula. One can now obtain the previously hidden signed correlation from readings at \(45^\circ\) and \(135^\circ\).

The experiment prepares an adjustable polarised fraction with azimuth \(\psi\) and ellipticity angle \(\chi\), mixed with an unpolarised background. Its normalised Stokes vector is \(P(\cos2\chi\cos2\psi,\cos2\chi\sin2\psi,\sin2\chi)\). Make the unassisted intensity scan flat by choosing circular polarisation, then insert the fixed retarder virtually and predict the recovered difference. Setting \(P=0\) makes both scans flat. This comparison shows which measurement discriminates the two cases.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on instrument design

A linear scan alone has \(I_{\max}=75,I_{\min}=25\). Give the range of total degrees of polarisation allowed and the maximum possible magnitude of \(S_3\).

Work through the solution

The linear magnitude is 50 and \(S_0=100\). Thus \(0.5\le P\le1\), and the positivity bound gives \(|S_3|\le\sqrt{100^2-50^2}=86.60\). A circular analyser measurement fixes the remaining magnitude and sign.

An ideal beam contains 60 units of pure horizontal light and 40 units of unpolarised light. Compare its Stokes vector with a coherent superposition of horizontal and vertical components with intensities 80 and 20 and zero relative phase.

Work through the solution

The incoherent mixture has \((100,60,0,0)\) and \(P=0.6\). The coherent preparation has \((100,60,80,0)\) since \(2\sqrt{80\times20}=80\), and \(P=1\). Their horizontal and vertical readings coincide. A diagonal analyser gives 50 for the mixture and 90 for the coherent preparation.

All three measured component differences equal 70 at measured total 100. Can a physical beam have these parameters?

Work through the solution

They imply \(|\boldsymbol S|=70\sqrt3=121.2>S_0\) and a negative matrix eigenvalue. Check dark subtraction, gain calibration, alignment and whether the source changed between readings. A physical fit with stated uncertainties is required before interpreting the data as an optical state.

Further reading · ETH Zürich, Stenflo on coherency and Stokes parameters.

Two polarisations moving through a uniaxial crystal

Electrodynamics

A crystal can send two pulses in different directions even when their wavefronts have the same normal. The distinction emerges by applying the divergence condition to electric displacement and measuring energy flow with the Poynting vector.

A field transverse to the wavevector need not be the electric field

Let a transparent, nonmagnetic uniaxial medium have \(\boldsymbol\epsilon/\epsilon_0=\operatorname{diag}(n_o^2,n_o^2,n_e^2)\). The optic axis is \(z\); dispersion over the bandwidth under study is negligible. For a harmonic plane wave with wavevector in the \(xz\) plane, write \(\mathbf k=(k_x,0,k_z)\). Source-free Maxwell equations require \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf D=0\), \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf B=0\), \(\mathbf k\times\mathbf E=\omega\mathbf B\) and \(\mathbf k\times\mathbf H=-\omega\mathbf D\). Eliminating the magnetic field gives

\[\mathbf k\times(\mathbf k\times\mathbf E)+k_0^2(\boldsymbol\epsilon/\epsilon_0)\mathbf E=0,\]
\[k_0=\omega/c.\]

An electric field along \(y\) satisfies this system with \(k^2=k_0^2n_o^2\). This ordinary mode has transverse \(\mathbf E,\mathbf D\) and energy travels parallel to \(\mathbf k\). A second mode has \(\mathbf E\) in the \(xz\) plane; \(\mathbf D\) is transverse but generally not parallel to \(\mathbf E\).

Find the phase index and the direction of energy separately

The remaining two Cartesian equations form a matrix with diagonal \(k_0^2 n_o^2-k_z^2,\ k_0^2 n_e^2-k_x^2\) and off-diagonal elements \(k_xk_z\). Its determinant vanishes for nontrivial fields. Expanding cancels the fourth-order cross term and gives

\[\frac{k_x^2}{n_e^2}+\frac{k_z^2}{n_o^2}=k_0^2,\]
\[\frac1{n_{\rm ph}(\theta)^2}=\frac{\sin^2\theta}{n_e^2}+\frac{\cos^2\theta}{n_o^2},\]

where \(\theta\) is the angle from the optic axis to the wavevector. Along the axis both modes have the same index; perpendicular to it the extraordinary phase index is \(n_e\). For the latter mode choose transverse \(\mathbf D\propto(\cos\theta,0,-\sin\theta)\). Applying the inverse permittivity tensor gives \(\mathbf E\propto(\cos\theta/n_o^2,0,-\sin\theta/n_e^2)\). The magnetic field is along \(y\), so the energy flow angle \(\psi\) from \(z\) satisfies

\[\tan\psi=-\frac{E_z}{E_x}=\frac{n_o^2}{n_e^2}\tan\theta.\]

The group velocity obtained by differentiating \(\omega(\mathbf k)=c\sqrt{k_x^2/n_e^2+k_z^2/n_o^2}\) has the same direction in this nondispersive model. This independent construction is normal to the constant-frequency ellipse. At an interface it is the tangential component of \(\mathbf k\), fixed by phase matching, that selects the transmitted wavevector. The energy path can then diverge from the corresponding wave normal.

A crystal plate as a beam displacer and as a retarder

A normally incident beam with an oblique optic axis

Let \(n_o=1.50,\ n_e=1.60\) and let the plate normal make \(45^\circ\) to the optic axis. At normal incidence the phase normal is the plate normal for both modes. For the extraordinary wave \(\psi=\arctan(2.25/2.56)=41.3^\circ\). Relative to its wave normal the energy travels about \(-3.7^\circ\) toward the optic axis. Across a 10.0 mm thick plate the lateral displacement relative to the ordinary beam is approximately 0.65 mm. This describes propagation inside a parallel plate and neglects edge clipping.

A plate that turns linear input into circular output

Propagate perpendicular to the optic axis so both eigenmodes travel in the same direction. Their accumulated phase difference is \(\Delta=k_0(n_e-n_o)d\). Linear incident polarisation at \(45^\circ\) to the eigenaxes has equal amplitudes. When \(|\Delta|=\pi/2\) modulo \(2\pi\), those orthogonal components have a quarter-period phase difference and form circular polarisation, assuming equal transmitted amplitudes. At 600 nm with \(n_e-n_o=0.10\), the smallest such thickness is \(1.50\) μm. A larger multi-order plate can give the same retardance at this wavelength but responds more strongly to bandwidth and thickness errors.

Recover retardance from a pair of polarisers

Place the crystal axes at \(45^\circ\) to the input polariser and use a second polariser crossed with the first. Express the output field in the two crystal axes, then project onto the crossed analyser; its amplitude is proportional to \((e^{i\Delta}-1)/2\). Thus the transmitted fraction is \(\sin^2(\Delta/2)\). An arbitrary phase offset common to both modes disappears. A single intensity reading determines neither the sign of \(\Delta\) nor its full order. Varying thickness or scanning wavelength can resolve the branch; the material dispersion then enters the interpretation.

Predict the transverse displacement of a crystal plate

Choose principal indices 1.50 and 1.60 and a 45° wave normal. The extraordinary beam is displaced by about −0.65 mm through a 10 mm plate; the ordinary beam follows the normal. Swap the principal indices and compare the sign and magnitude of walk-off.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Problem sheet · use energy and phase observations

Infer birefringence from a walk-off angle

At \(\theta=45^\circ\), an extraordinary energy ray makes \(\psi=40^\circ\) to the optic axis. Assuming the same model, recover \(n_e/n_o\). Is the medium positive or negative uniaxial?

Work through the solution

The ratio is \(\sqrt{\tan45^\circ/\tan40^\circ}=1.092\). The extraordinary principal index exceeds the ordinary one, a positive uniaxial material. A measurement referenced to the plate normal must first be converted to the angle from the optic axis used in the equation.

Calculate the contrast loss caused by angular misalignment

An ideal half-wave plate has its eigenaxis at angle \(\alpha\) to the input polariser. Keep the analyser crossed and find the intensity transmitted when the angle is \(40^\circ\) instead of \(45^\circ\).

Work through the solution

Projection into the crystal axes and back out gives \(I/I_0=\sin^2(2\alpha)\sin^2(\Delta/2)\). At half-wave retardance the factor from phase is unity, and the misaligned transmission is \(\sin^2 80^\circ=0.970\). The reduction alone cannot distinguish angular error from a small error in retardance.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, 5th ed., §§4.3–4.4 and 9.1.4 · constitutive laws and polarisation
  • Fließbach, chapter 33 · wave solutions in media

What a momentum eigenstate means in a finite experiment

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

A detector never counts a perfectly sharp momentum eigenket. Derive continuum normalisation from a finite box, change the spectral measure from momentum to energy and predict the full evolution of a normalisable Gaussian packet.

Follow a normalised state as its allowed box grows

Consider a particle moving in one spatial dimension. A periodic box of length \(L\) is a mathematical model for an observation made far from any reflecting wall. The inner product is the integral of \(\psi^*(x)\chi(x)\) over the box. The momentum operator is \(-i\hbar\,d/dx\) with periodic boundary conditions. Integration by parts cancels its boundary form for periodic differentiable functions. Its normalised modes \(L^{-1/2}e^{ik_n x}\) have \(k_n=2\pi n/L\) and momenta \(p_n=\hbar k_n\). Their overlap is one if the integers coincide and zero otherwise. Increasing \(L\) reduces the spacing \(\Delta p=2\pi\hbar/L\) but also reduces the amplitude of each individual box mode at a fixed position. If one simply replaces the Kronecker delta by a Dirac delta while carrying over \(L^{-1/2}\), the units and probabilities will fail.

Let \(c_n\) be the unit-normalised amplitudes with \(\sum_n|c_n|^2=1\). For a smooth normalisable packet the probability in an interval \(dp\) has a limit \(|\phi(p)|^2 dp\). Thus \(c_n\simeq\sqrt{\Delta p}\,\phi(p_n)\). Substituting in the synthesis sum and using \(L\Delta p=2\pi\hbar\) produces

\[\psi(x)=\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{ipx/\hbar}\phi(p)\,dp,\qquad\]
\[\phi(p)=\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi\hbar}}\int_{-\infty}^\infty e^{-ipx/\hbar}\psi(x)\,dx .\]

The distributions \(\langle x|p\rangle=(2\pi\hbar)^{-1/2}e^{ipx/\hbar}\) have \(\langle p|p'\rangle=\delta(p-p')\). The delta acts under integration; \(\delta(0)\) has no finite interpretation as the probability for a detector. A physical probability is \(\int_{p_1}^{p_2}|\phi(p)|^2dp\). Changing to wave number gives \(|\phi_k(k)|^2=\hbar|\phi(\hbar k)|^2\). A graph labelled density in \(k\) with a vertical axis copied directly from the momentum plot loses the Jacobian and in general ceases to normalise to one.

Change from momentum bins to energy bins

For a free particle \(E=p^2/(2m)\). Each positive energy has two distinct momenta \(p_\pm=\pm\sqrt{2mE}\). A detector measuring energy but not direction sums over these mutually orthogonal alternatives. Apply the ordinary change-of-variables rule on each monotone half-line:

\[w(E)=\frac{m}{\sqrt{2mE}}\left[|\phi(+\sqrt{2mE})|^2+|\phi(-\sqrt{2mE})|^2\right],\]
\[E>0.\]

The factor \(m/|p|\) is \(1/|dE/dp|\), with units of momentum per energy. Measuring a finite energy interval around zero can have a density diverging as \(E^{-1/2}\) if \(\phi(0)\ne0\), yet the integrated probability is finite because \(\int_0^\delta E^{-1/2}dE=2\sqrt\delta\). The same effect appears in a box as relatively dense low-energy modes. Standing-wave box boundary conditions change the counting of travelling directions; include degeneracy once and use the appropriate mode spacing, rather than doubling both.

On an infinite line it is useful to introduce \(|E,+\rangle=\sqrt{m/|p|}\,|p\rangle\) at \(p>0\), and a corresponding negative branch. In the overlap, \(\delta(p(E)-p(E'))=\delta(E-E')/|dp/dE|\); the two square-root factors give \(\delta(E-E')\). The resolution of identity contains \(\sum_{\pm}\int_0^\infty dE\,|E,\pm\rangle\langle E,\pm|\). Measuring energy while coherently manipulating the two degenerate directions requires keeping both amplitudes. Adding their probabilities is sufficient only for an instrument whose outcome identifies energy without mixing the directions before detection.

Integrate the free evolution of a Gaussian

Prepare a minimum-uncertainty wave packet centred at \(x_0\) with average momentum \(p_0\) and initial position standard deviation \(s\):

\[\psi(x,0)=(2\pi s^2)^{-1/4}\exp[-(x-x_0)^2/(4s^2)+ip_0(x-x_0)/\hbar].\]

Its squared modulus is a Gaussian with variance \(s^2\), which explains the factor four in the amplitude exponent. Complete the square in the Fourier integral to find a momentum probability Gaussian centred at \(p_0\) of variance \(\hbar^2/(4s^2)\). The initial state has zero symmetrised position–momentum covariance. Multiplying by a chirp factor \(\exp[i\kappa(x-x_0)^2]\) leaves its initial position density unchanged but changes momentum variance and covariance. A snapshot of one density does not determine its later free spread.

Each momentum component evolves by \(e^{-ip^2t/(2m\hbar)}\). Split \(p=p_0+q\). The constant term in \(p^2\) produces a common phase; the linear term shifts the envelope by \(p_0t/m\); the quadratic term changes the width. Carrying out the remaining Gaussian integral yields

\[|\psi(x,t)|^2=\frac{\exp[-(x-x_0-p_0t/m)^2/(2s_t^2)]}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,s_t},\]
\[s_t^2=s^2\left[1+\left(\frac{\hbar t}{2ms^2}\right)^2\right].\]

An independent route uses the Heisenberg operator \(x(t)=x(0)+p(0)t/m\). Its variance is \(s^2+(t/m)\langle\{\Delta x,\Delta p\}\rangle+(t/m)^2(\Delta p)^2\). Inserting zero covariance and the Gaussian momentum width reproduces \(s_t^2\). For a chirped packet the covariance term can be negative; momentary narrowing during free evolution is consistent with uncertainty because momentum width and the phase correlation must be included. The density integral stays one, so spreading lowers the height of the peak without removing particles.

Take a model atom of mass \(1.44\times10^{-25}\) kg prepared with \(s=1.00\,\mu\mathrm m\). Its spread time \(t_s=2ms^2/\hbar=2.73\) ms. At \(t=t_s\) the standard deviation is 1.41 μm. With mean velocity 2.00 mm/s its mean has advanced 5.46 μm. At long flight times \(s_t\simeq t\Delta p/m\). Measuring position after a known long flight therefore maps the momentum distribution for this free packet. At \(0.5t_s\) the neglected initial position variance is still dominant, so the same inference would substantially overestimate \(\Delta p\).

A detector window and a Fourier grid do different jobs
