Suppose the position-sensitive detector has a Gaussian point-spread function of width \(d\). The measured density is a convolution of the true density with that response if detections are independent and gain is uniform. Gaussian variances then add: \(s_{\rm obs}^2=s_t^2+d^2\). This broadening belongs to the observation model; it does not increase the particle's momentum spread before the measurement. For \(d=1.20\,\mu\mathrm m\) at \(t=t_s\), the observed standard deviation becomes \(1.85\,\mu\mathrm m\). A fit to a single image with unknown \(d\) cannot disentangle the two contributions. A sequence of flight times constrains the coefficient of \(t^2\) independently of a fixed instrument width.

A discrete Fourier propagation represents a periodic interval sampled at spacing \(\Delta x\). Its wave-number spacing is \(2\pi/L\), and the highest resolved wave number is order \(\pi/\Delta x\). Enlarging \(L\) at fixed \(\Delta x\) samples the momentum profile more finely and delays periodic wraparound. Reducing \(\Delta x\) at fixed \(L\) raises the bandwidth without moving the periodic boundary. Either operation alone can leave the other error intact. A norm-preserving FFT propagator can therefore return a perfectly normalised wave packet that has wrapped through an unphysical boundary or whose carrier momentum has aliased. Compare the phase, group velocity and width against independent formulas, then refine the two scales separately.

Why boundary conditions are part of a momentum observable

The expression \(p=-i\hbar\,d/dx\) does not by itself specify a complete operator. On a finite interval \([0,L]\), integrate two sufficiently smooth square-integrable functions by parts:

\[\langle f,pg\rangle-\langle pf,g\rangle=-i\hbar[f^*(x)g(x)]_{0}^{L}.\]

A symmetric domain makes this boundary form vanish for every pair in the domain. A self-adjoint operator additionally has the same domain as its adjoint. This distinction matters for a physical generator of unitary translations and for assigning sharp spectral outcomes. Imposing a real or imaginary matrix representation in a finite basis can conceal boundary conditions that become essential as the basis is enlarged.

For a ring allow quasi-periodicity \(g(L)=e^{i\theta}g(0)\) with fixed \(\theta\) common to all functions in the domain and square-integrable first derivative. Then the boundary form vanishes. Conversely, for an adjoint candidate \(f\) the requirement that it vanish for all possible \(g(0)\) forces \(f(L)=e^{i\theta}f(0)\), so the same condition reappears. Solving \(p g=pg\) yields the momenta \(p_n=\hbar(2\pi n+\theta)/L\). Thus boxes with precisely the same local differential expression have momentum spectra shifted by an externally fixed phase. In a physically realised ring such a phase can encode a magnetic flux through a gauge-transformed boundary condition; a gauge consistent with that interpretation must also be used in the Hamiltonian and physical momentum.

Compare with forcing \(g(0)=g(L)=0\) as in a hard-wall well. The boundary form vanishes for all \(f,g\) satisfying both zero conditions, so the derivative is symmetric there. But the adjoint boundary form vanishes for arbitrary endpoint values of \(f\) when \(g\) vanishes at both ends. Consequently this two-zero derivative domain is smaller than its adjoint domain. There is no ordinary rigid translation that carries every state of a hard-wall interval back into that same interval without assigning what happens at its boundary. Meanwhile the kinetic energy \(-\hbar^2d^2/(2m\,dx^2)\) with suitable Dirichlet domain is self-adjoint and has a well-defined discrete spectrum. The symbol \(p^2/(2m)\) in a Dirichlet well does not turn its first derivative with the same two endpoint conditions into a self-adjoint sharp momentum observable.

A release-and-detect experiment makes this distinction concrete. Prepare the first Dirichlet state \(\sqrt{2/L}\sin(\pi x/L)\) supported in \(0<x<L\), then remove the walls suddenly and measure momentum on the full line. Its momentum wavefunction is the Fourier transform of the spatial function extended by zero outside. Multiplying the exponential and sine and integrating gives a continuous amplitude proportional to \([1+e^{-ipL/\hbar}]/[(\pi/L)^2-(p/\hbar)^2]\). The apparent singularities at \(p/\hbar=\pm\pi/L\) are removable because the numerator also vanishes. It is a mistake to predict two delta peaks by replacing the standing wave with the sum of two infinite plane waves. The finite support multiplies that sum by a spatial window and broadens both momentum components.

The mean momentum for this real function is zero. Its second moment after release is \(\hbar^2\int|\psi'|^2dx=\pi^2\hbar^2/L^2\), consistent with the original kinetic energy. Thus a continuous released momentum distribution can have an rms momentum whose square exactly recovers a discrete energy of the original bounded system. This is a useful quantitative example where the equality of moments across two measurement procedures does not imply equality of their full spectral probability distributions. Adding a short laboratory impulse to every particle after release should translate the whole distribution by the corresponding momentum kick, preserving its width. That response provides an independent check on a momentum detector's sign and scale.

Derive the free propagator with a convergent limiting prescription

Partitioning the free unitary into momentum eigenstates produces its position kernel

\[K(x,t;x',0)=\frac1{2\pi\hbar}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(p(x-x')-\frac{p^2t}{2m}\right)\right]dp .\]

It is oscillatory rather than absolutely integrable. Define it by first damping \(e^{-\varepsilon p^2}\), evaluating a convergent Gaussian and then taking \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) on smooth normalisable test packets. Completing the square in the exponent gives, for \(t>0\),

\[K=\sqrt{\frac{m}{2\pi i\hbar t}}\exp\left[\frac{im(x-x')^2}{2\hbar t}\right].\]

The phase convention of the square root for positive time follows continuously from the regularised Gaussian. The units are inverse length so \(\psi(x,t)=\int K(x,t;x',0)\psi(x',0)\,dx'\) has the same units as the initial wavefunction. As \(t\to0^+\), the kernel does not approach a finite spike pointwise. Its increasingly fast oscillations across \(x'\) cancel except near \(x'=x\), recovering the initial state in the appropriate strong or distributional sense. For a constant initial amplitude one may recover unity formally, but such an amplitude on the full line is itself not a normalised test state.

Two checks involve no wave packet fitting. Inserting two kernels for \(t_1,t_2\) and integrating over an intermediate position gives the kernel for \(t_1+t_2\), either by another Gaussian integration or, more directly, by composing the momentum phase factors. Complex conjugation and reversing the time arguments yield the inverse evolution. Neither statement holds for an arbitrary choice of magnitude prefactor assigned merely to obtain the correct phase. In Fourier numerics the corresponding checks are unit norm after repeated evolution and recovery of the original sampled complex state after a forward followed by backward propagation. These are necessary algorithm checks, even though a periodic or aliased computational model can still satisfy them exactly.

This kernel also clarifies the long-time momentum mapping. Factor \(e^{im x^2/(2\hbar t)}\) from \(K\) and identify the Fourier transform of \(e^{imx'^2/(2\hbar t)}\psi(x',0)\) evaluated at \(p=mx/t\). If the occupied initial region is narrow enough that \(mx'^2/(2\hbar t)\) varies little across it about the selected origin, the extra phase can be neglected and

\[|\psi(x,t)|^2\simeq\frac mt\left|\phi\!\left(\frac{mx}{t}\right)\right|^2 .\]

The factor \(m/t\) is the Jacobian \(dp/dx\). When the initial packet is displaced, use a suitable shifted origin or account for the induced linear phase explicitly. This derivation applies well beyond Gaussians and gives a scale for testing the approximation before interpreting a flight image as a momentum histogram. The quadratic residual phase provides the matter-wave analogue of the near-field correction in diffraction.

Distinguish a detector crossing from a snapshot in space

A detector counts particles passing through an aperture over time. Its signal is related to flux, whereas a camera image of identical preparations at one time probes a position distribution. For a one-dimensional free wavefunction the continuity equation is \(\partial_t|\psi|^2+\partial_xj=0\) with \(j=(\hbar/m)\operatorname{Im}(\psi^*\partial_x\psi)\). Integrating over the region \(x<X\) gives the change in probability to the left of a fixed plane. If there is no flux at negative infinity, the net integrated current through \(X\) equals the reduction in that probability. A positive-reading counter with absorption is a more detailed instrument and cannot in general be identified with a signed current in the presence of reflected waves or interference.

For a minimum-uncertainty Gaussian initially at \(x_0\), the exact phase in the freely expanding envelope has a quadratic term. Differentiating the phase gives the local velocity entering current,

\[\frac{j(x,t)}{|\psi(x,t)|^2}=\frac{p_0}{m}+\frac{t}{t_s^2+t^2}\left(x-x_0-\frac{p_0t}{m}\right).\]

At the centre this equals the group velocity \(p_0/m\), but ahead of the centre it is larger and behind it smaller. A single constant velocity multiplying density is inadequate for a significantly spreading packet. For the earlier atom at \(t=t_s\), one current-standard-deviation distance ahead corresponds to \(1.41\,\mu\mathrm m\) and the additional speed is about \(0.259\) mm/s. The trailing side gives the opposite correction. Direct differentiation of its Gaussian density verifies that this current satisfies continuity. A code reproducing \(s_t\) but evolving the wrong complex phase may fail this current test; judging an entire propagation by the changing width alone would miss the error.

A useful inverse question is whether two time-of-flight images suffice to recover an unknown complex wavefunction. In the long-time limit the image yields \(|\phi(p)|^2\), which contains no direct momentum-dependent phase. Translation of the initial wave packet changes \(\phi(p)\) by a linear phase, leaving the momentum density unchanged. Complex conjugate or chirped preparations can also share particular intensity records. Additional calibrated evolution, known reference interference or structural assumptions can provide phase information. Even an exact free propagator only defines the forward map; its invertibility on complex amplitudes does not imply intensity-only data identify every possible input. The experiment must specify which class of preparations and which measurement settings render the desired parameters estimable.

Track a continuous superposition through focus

Change a position-momentum correlation and compare the full packet width with an initially unchirped packet. A narrow width at one time does not determine its subsequent spread.

Open the live experiment

Plan an FFT comparison using physical position and momentum resolution

A grid propagator can track the same packet analytically solved earlier, then carry over its verified conventions to less elementary potentials. A common trap is to regard the number of points \(N\) as a single accuracy parameter. Define a periodic interval of physical length \(L=N\Delta x\). Discrete Fourier modes have \(k_j=2\pi j/L\), with negative wave numbers represented by negative indices after reordering. Their spacing is \(\Delta k=2\pi/L\); the largest distinct magnitude is close to \(\pi/\Delta x\). A narrow structure in momentum requires large \(L\). A high carrier momentum requires small \(\Delta x\). These conditions can fail independently even when the norm stays exactly one.

If \(\psi_l=\psi(x_l)\) is a sampled wavefunction in inverse square-root metres and the forward transform sums without \(1/N\), the approximated continuum \(k\)-amplitude is \(\phi_k(k_j)\simeq\Delta x\,\sum_l\psi_l e^{-ik_jx_l}/\sqrt{2\pi}\). The phase includes the physical coordinate origin adopted for the grid. Parseval then gives \(\Delta k\sum_j|\phi_k(k_j)|^2=\Delta x\sum_l|\psi_l|^2\). A library returning a unitary discrete transform instead assigns a factor \(1/\sqrt N\) to both directions; multiplying its output by \(\sqrt{\Delta x/\Delta k}\) converts sample units. Plotting a library output directly against physical momentum without these scale factors is especially misleading when comparing two meshes. Total discrete \(\ell^2\) norm and continuum probability density are different normalisations of the same samples.

For the atom of mass \(1.44\times10^{-25}\) kg, \(s=1.00\) μm and \(v_0=2.00\) mm/s, \(k_0=mv_0/\hbar=2.73\,\mu\mathrm m^{-1}\) and \(\Delta k_{\rm rms}=1/(2s)=0.50\,\mu\mathrm m^{-1}\). A box 80 μm wide gives spacing \(0.0785\,\mu\mathrm m^{-1}\), about 6.4 modes per rms momentum width. With \(N=256\), grid spacing is 0.3125 μm and Nyquist wavenumber is \(10.05\,\mu\mathrm m^{-1}\), exceeding \(k_0+5\Delta k_{\rm rms}=5.23\,\mu\mathrm m^{-1}\). This comfortably samples the initial carrier distribution. It is still necessary to check that the envelope remains inside the periodic box at the desired final time.

At \(t=10\) ms the packet centre has moved 20 μm and \(s_t\simeq3.80\) μm. Starting at the centre of an interval spanning \(-40\) to \(40\) μm, its five-standard-deviation front approaches \(39\) μm and begins testing the boundary margin. At 20 ms the centre alone reaches the periodic boundary. A perfectly unitary calculation will show it reappearing on the opposite side. This is correct for a ring and incorrect for a free-space flight image. Enlarging \(N\) while keeping the same \(L\) improves the Nyquist limit but does nothing to postpone this crossing. An open-boundary absorber can delay reflected artefacts but attenuates norm; a lost norm in that model should be compared with counted escape rather than naively enforced to zero.

Infer displacement and speed from a wrapped packet without mistaking periodicity for reflection

A code on a periodic box of length 80 μm reports a sharp packet peak at \(+35\) μm in one frame and \(-36\) μm in the next frame 4.50 ms later. Find a consistent uniform drift and identify the ambiguity if no speed prior is available.

Derive and check the result

The smallest forward displacement is \(+9\) μm after adding one full box length to the second position. It corresponds to \(2.00\) mm/s and agrees with the earlier preparation. Direct subtraction without unwrapping would give \(-71\) μm, or \(-15.8\) mm/s, an apparent reversal. All displacements \(9+80n\) μm for integer \(n\) yield the same wrapped pair, so the frames alone cannot identify the number of turns. A momentum measurement or a bound on speed relative to frame spacing resolves the branch. For an extended packet crossing a seam, use a periodic circular moment such as \(\langle e^{2\pi i x/L}\rangle\) so an ordinary spatial mean does not spuriously jump toward the box centre. If that circular moment is nearly zero, even its angular centre is ill-defined and a full density comparison is preferable.

Tutorial · design a momentum measurement

Distinguish an unresolved line from a finite-width packet

A periodic calculation of length \(L=100\,\mu\mathrm m\) contains mode \(n\). A detector bins momentum in intervals \(1.0\times10^{-29}\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\). How many box-mode spacings fit into a bin? What changes if the box length doubles at fixed physical packet?

Work through the solution

Here \(\Delta p=h/L=6.626\times10^{-30}\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}\), so the detector bin spans about 1.51 spacings. The precise number of grid points falling within a bin depends on its endpoints. Doubling \(L\) gives roughly 3.02 spacings per bin. Each corresponding coefficient squared decreases approximately in proportion to \(\Delta p\), while the number included increases. The total probability should approach a stable integral on a progressively finer grid. If a graph compares \(|c_n|^2\) across the boxes, divide by the corresponding \(\Delta p\) before calling the ordinate a continuum density.

Calculate a threshold energy interval without evaluating a delta at zero

A packet's momentum density is nearly constant \(w_0\) over \(-p_c<p<p_c\). Obtain the probability for \(0<E<E_c=p_c^2/(2m)\) by two different integrations. What changes when only positive-momentum particles reach the counter?

Work through the solution

Direct integration in momentum gives \(2p_c w_0\). In energy the two branches contribute \(2mw_0/\sqrt{2mE}\). Its integral gives \(4mw_0\sqrt{E_c}/\sqrt{2m}=2p_cw_0\) as required. A positive-direction selector halves this particular probability when applied to the original ensemble. If the entire subsequent experiment is renormalised to the selected particles, divide again by the total positive-momentum probability. Those two reported probabilities correspond to different conditioning and should be labelled explicitly.

Infer the packet width from two images

The model atom has an initial measured width 1.56 μm and a width 1.85 μm after 2.73 ms. Assume a time-independent Gaussian instrument response and a freely evolving unchirped minimum-uncertainty state. Infer \(s\) and \(d\), and identify an additional check.

Work through the solution

Subtract squared widths to remove the detector: \(s_{\rm obs}(t)^2-s_{\rm obs}(0)^2=\hbar^2t^2/(4m^2s^2)\). The observed difference is about \(0.99\,\mu{\rm m}^2\), giving \(s\simeq1.0\,\mu\mathrm m\) and \(d=\sqrt{s_{\rm obs}(0)^2-s^2}\simeq1.2\,\mu\mathrm m\), within rounding. A third flight time tests the predicted quadratic in \(t\). A term linear in time would point to a nonzero initial correlation or to measurement drift. Independent calibration of the point-spread function distinguishes those interpretations from a nonminimum momentum width.

Further reading

  • Fließbach · Eigenwerte und Eigenfunktionen

From a finite pulse to a continuum transition rate

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

Switch a weak perturbation on for a measured duration. Its Fourier transform determines the amplitude of a discrete transition. Summing over a dense set of final states reveals where a time-independent transition rate comes from and where it fails.

Remove the known free evolution before approximating

Let \(H_0|n\rangle=E_n|n\rangle\) and \(H(t)=H_0+V(t)\). Expand \(|\psi(t)\rangle=\sum_n c_n(t)e^{-iE_n t/\hbar}|n\rangle\). Substituting into the time-dependent Schrödinger equation cancels the free phase derivatives and leaves an exact equation \(i\hbar\dot c_f=\sum_n e^{i(E_f-E_n)t/\hbar} V_{fn}(t)c_n(t)\). Suppose the initial state is \(|i\rangle\) and the perturbation is appreciable only between \(0\) and \(T\). Iterating the integral equation once, replacing \(c_n(t)\) on the right by \(\delta_{ni}\), yields

\[c_f^{(1)}(T)=-\frac i\hbar\int_0^T e^{i\omega_{fi}t}V_{fi}(t)\,dt,\]
\[\omega_{fi}=(E_f-E_i)/\hbar .\]

For \(f\ne i\) the leading transition probability is \(|c_f^{(1)}|^2\). It is second order in the perturbation even though the amplitude is first order. Conservation of total probability to this order requires a second-order correction to the initial amplitude. Simply appending all positive final probabilities while keeping the initial probability exactly one would fail that test. The approximation is controlled when total probability removed from the initial state is small over the observation duration and higher-order pathways are negligible.

Let the resonant component of a drive have matrix element \(V_{fi}(t)=v e^{-i\omega t}\) for \(0<t<T\). For a real cosine perturbation the two exponentials each carry half the real peak amplitude, so \(v\) refers to just the near-resonant part. Define \(\Delta=\omega_{fi}-\omega\). Direct integration gives

\[P_f^{(1)}=\frac{|v|^2T^2}{\hbar^2}\operatorname{sinc}^2(\Delta T/2),\]
\[\operatorname{sinc}x=\sin x/x .\]

At resonance the amplitude grows linearly in time while its phase remains coherent. A detuned contribution winds around in the complex plane and largely cancels. The first zeros occur at ordinary detuning \(\pm1/T\) in hertz. The full width at half height is approximately \(0.886/T\) in ordinary frequency since \(\sin x/x=1/\sqrt2\) at \(x\simeq1.3916\). Doubling duration quadruples the line-centre probability for one final state but halves the linewidth, a distinction central to interpreting a continuum rate.

Sum over final states before taking a long-time limit

A large box provides a discrete set of final levels with spacing \(\delta E\); at fixed finite time many can lie inside the pulse's energy bandwidth. Let \(\rho(E)\) be the density of the accessible final states per joule, including degeneracy exactly once. Replace the sum by an integral with \(E_*=E_i+\hbar\omega\):

\[P_{\rm out}\simeq\frac{T^2}{\hbar^2}\int dE\,\rho(E)|v(E)|^2\operatorname{sinc}^2\left[\frac{(E-E_*)T}{2\hbar}\right].\]

If \(\rho|v|^2\) changes little across the dominant width and the line is well away from thresholds, take it outside the integral. Substitute \(u=(E-E_*)T/(2\hbar)\), and use \(\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sin u/u)^2du=\pi\). The area of the squared Fourier transform grows linearly, giving

\[\Gamma=\frac{P_{\rm out}}T\simeq\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}|v(E_*)|^2\rho(E_*).\]

The identity for the sinc area also follows by Parseval from the energy in the rectangular time window. This is Fermi's golden rule with \(\rho\) per energy. If a supplied density is per unit angular frequency, \(\rho_\omega=\hbar\rho_E\); failing to change that measure introduces a factor of \(\hbar\). The units are a strong check: energy squared times states per energy divided by action is inverse seconds.

Several limits have been taken in a definite order. The perturbation lasts long enough to resolve the scale on which \(\rho|v|^2\) varies but short enough that many levels still fit in its bandwidth. A window of times must exist between a reservoir correlation time and a recurrence time of order \(2\pi\hbar/\delta E\). In addition \(\Gamma T\ll1\) for the first-order depletion argument. Taking \(T\to\infty\) at fixed finite box instead resolves individual levels and can produce coherent oscillations or recurrences. Extending the constant rate to a full exponential decay uses a further memoryless approximation and renewed weak transitions of the remaining population. A linear short-time fit by itself does not establish exponential dynamics at every time.

Keep matrix elements and density normalisation consistent

For illustration choose \(|v|=5.00\times10^{-6}\) eV and \(\rho=20.0\ \mathrm{eV^{-1}}\) for box-normalised accessible modes dense enough on the pulse scale, giving \(\Gamma\simeq4.77\times10^6\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\) as a formal product. But the implied mean level spacing \(1/\rho=0.050\) eV corresponds to a recurrence scale of only \(8.27\times10^{-14}\) s. Using a nanosecond pulse with this literal finite density violates the continuum criterion, irrespective of how small \(\Gamma T\) looks. This numerical mismatch is a useful trap: reproducing a golden-rule number to many digits does not ensure the system supports irreversible decay in the chosen time range.

Now enlarge the quantisation volume by \(10^6\) for continuum-normalised physical coupling. The number of accessible states per energy grows by \(10^6\), while each squared matrix element shrinks by \(10^6\) for a localised initial state and individually unit-normalised extended final states. The same product gives the same \(\Gamma\), but spacing is now \(5.0\times10^{-8}\) eV and the recurrence scale 82.7 ns. At \(T=2.0\) ns, \(\Gamma T=0.00954\) and roughly \(h/(T\delta E)\simeq41\) spacings fit up to the first positive zero. Whether density and coupling are locally smooth on that bandwidth remains an additional condition. The scaling removes explicit box dependence from a physical rate while clarifying why direct sums at finite volume can require larger spaces.

Use phase space to predict the shape of a continuum edge

For free relative motion in three dimensions, count single scalar internal channels in a cubic periodic volume \(\mathcal V\). One state occupies volume \((2\pi)^3/\mathcal V\) in wave-vector space. The shell between \(k,k+dk\) thus contains \(\mathcal V4\pi k^2dk/(2\pi)^3\). From \(E=\hbar^2k^2/(2m)\) it follows that \(\rho(E)=\mathcal V m k/(2\pi^2\hbar^2)\), which vanishes like \(\sqrt E\) at threshold. In one dimension the corresponding free density diverges like \(1/\sqrt E\); the geometrical shell measure is different. A threshold suppression observed in one spectrum could come from available states, the coupling matrix element, or both.

If a local transition couples chiefly to a final orbital partial wave \(l\), its regular low-\(k\) free radial factor near the source scales as \(k^l\) when the potential is sufficiently short ranged and no threshold resonance changes the law. Squaring and multiplying by the three-dimensional density gives \(\Gamma_l\propto k^{2l+1}\propto E^{l+1/2}\). For a \(p\)-wave channel doubling the excess energy increases this ideal rate by \(2^{3/2}=2.83\). Long-range Coulomb fields alter the final-state radial behaviour and can invalidate the short-range threshold power. Very close to any edge a finite pulse straddles energies where the density changes rapidly or vanishes. Replace the local golden-rule product by the finite-window integral to model the measured onset.

Compare pulse shapes at equal area and equal energy

The rectangular pulse is useful because its Fourier transform is elementary. Its abrupt start and end also generate sidelobes that can excite nearby unwanted transitions. For a general narrowband drive write its resonant matrix element \(v(t)e^{-i\omega t}\). First order gives \(P_f=|\int v(t)e^{i\Delta t}dt|^2/\hbar^2\). At zero detuning only the complex pulse area \(\int v(t)dt\) matters. After summing over a flat continuum, Parseval gives \(\int P_f(\Delta)d\Delta=(2\pi/\hbar^2)\int|v(t)|^2dt\), so it is the temporal integral of squared coupling that controls total spectral weight. Two pulses with the same area can have different continuum probabilities; two with the same integrated intensity can have different resonant peak heights.

For a Gaussian envelope \(v(t)=v_0e^{-t^2/(2\tau^2)}\) on the full line, complete the square in its Fourier integral. The amplitude is \(v_0\sqrt{2\pi}\tau e^{-\Delta^2\tau^2/2}\), so the transition profile has no sidelobes and has full half-height angular width \(2\sqrt{\ln2}/\tau\). The time intensity is \(|v_0|^2e^{-t^2/\tau^2}\), with temporal full half-height width \(2\sqrt{\ln2}\tau\). Multiplying this by the ordinary-frequency full width gives \(2\ln2/\pi=0.441\). Replacing an amplitude width by an intensity width changes that product by \(\sqrt2\); specifying the plotted quantity is necessary when comparing a spectrum with temporal photodetector data.

Take a Gaussian with \(\tau=0.50\) ms. Its spectral intensity full width is \(530\) Hz and pulse intensity full temporal width \(0.833\) ms. An unwanted line 1.0 kHz away receives a relative first-order probability \(e^{-(2\pi1000\tau)^2}\simeq5.17\times10^{-5}\). A nominally same-duration rectangular pulse may place an ideal zero near some precisely chosen detuning, but neighbouring frequencies generally see sidelobes. Which pulse gives smaller unwanted excitation depends on what quantity is fixed, where the extra lines lie and how much uncertainty there is in their positions. A shaped envelope can suppress spectral leakage without improving every possible resolution metric.

A pair of pulses illustrates how coherently adding amplitudes differs from broadening a single envelope. Let identical, short pulses of area \(A\) be separated by start-time difference \(T_s\), and let the second have a controlled drive phase \(\phi\). At a detuning small compared with each pulse bandwidth, the amplitude is proportional to \(A[1+e^{i(\Delta T_s+\phi)}]\). Its squared magnitude has fringes \(2|A|^2[1+\cos(\Delta T_s+\phi)]\) inside the broader single-pulse line. Their ordinary-frequency spacing \(1/T_s\) can be much smaller than the inverse individual pulse duration. Randomising the relative phase removes fringes while keeping the two single-pulse energies unchanged. The observed fine spectral structure is therefore a measurement of coherent time separation as well as energy difference.

Derive depletion and the energy shift from the same kernel

The first-order rate gives initial population loss in a useful time window. To see which extra assumptions allow exponential decay, retain a single initial discrete level coupled to many initially empty final levels with time-independent couplings \(v_f\) in a rotating frame. Let \(c_i(0)=1,\ c_f(0)=0\). In the interaction picture \(\dot c_f=-(i/\hbar)v_f e^{i\omega_{fi}t}c_i(t)\). Integrate this equation exactly in terms of the unknown \(c_i\) and insert the result in its partner equation for \(\dot c_i\). Assuming final levels do not couple directly to one another gives

\[\dot c_i(t)=-\int_0^t K(t-t')c_i(t')\,dt',\]
\[K(\tau)=\hbar^{-2}\sum_f |v_f|^2e^{-i\omega_{fi}\tau}.\]

No irreversibility has been assumed in this elimination. The previous discrete amplitudes are encoded in a memory kernel and can feed back into the initial level. At very short times \(c_i(t)\simeq1-K(0)t^2/2\) when the second moment is finite, yielding a quadratic survival loss. At long times a sharply structured or bounded continuum can produce nonexponential decay. A finite number of exactly known final levels evolves unitarily under the full Hamiltonian and can show recurrences; treating the memory as identically zero after any arbitrarily short interval would discard this possibility.

When the spectral coupling varies slowly over the line and extends across a sufficiently broad band, phase cancellation makes \(K(\tau)\) decay over a correlation time \(\tau_c\). For \(\tau_c\) short relative to changes in \(c_i\), write \(c_i(t-\tau)\approx c_i(t)\) inside its memory integral after the initial transient, and extend its upper limit to infinity. The regulated integral \(\int_0^\infty e^{-i\omega\tau-\eta\tau}d\tau=(\eta+i\omega)^{-1}\) tends to \(\pi\delta(\omega)-i\,\mathcal P(1/\omega)\) as a distribution. Its real part gives \(\dot c_i=-\Gamma c_i/2+\cdots\), with the same golden-rule population rate \(\Gamma\). The imaginary part gives a coherent energy shift proportional to \(\mathcal P\sum_f |v_f|^2/(E_i-E_f)\), requiring the physical bandwidth and a consistent bare energy. This shows how an absorptive linewidth and a dispersive shift can emerge from different parts of the same elimination, without claiming a perfectly flat infinite continuum supplies a physically meaningful absolute shift.

For a symmetric model with constant spectral strength inside \(-W<\omega_{fi}<W\), the kernel is proportional to \(\sin(W\tau)/\tau\). Narrowing the band increases its memory time and can delay the onset of an approximately constant rate. Moving the discrete level close to a band edge also removes the cancellation between positive and negative detunings responsible for a vanishing symmetric shift. An experiment that varies the carrier across such an edge should fit the full finite-window spectrum before inferring a time-independent linewidth or level shift. A numerically cheap sum over known discrete final energies provides a check: double the spectral cutoff, halve level spacing and track the early quadratic regime separately from any intermediate exponential segment.

Ask whether frequent projective tests inhibit a transition

For an initial pure state with finite energy variance under a time-independent \(H\), expand the survival amplitude \(A(t)=\langle i|e^{-iHt/\hbar}|i\rangle\) to second order. The linear term \(-it\langle H\rangle/\hbar\) is a phase. Squaring gives \(P_i(t)=1-(\Delta H)^2t^2/\hbar^2+O(t^3)\), with still higher-order control depending on available moments. Define \(t_Z=\hbar/\Delta H\). In the model with one initial level coupled only off-diagonally to the final levels, \((\Delta H)^2=\sum_f|v_f|^2\), agreeing with the memory-kernel expansion. If the needed moments diverge in an idealised continuum, this short-time expansion requires a physical high-frequency regularisation.

Now make ideal instantaneous projective tests of whether the system is still in \(|i\rangle\) every \(\tau=T/N\), selecting only histories that have passed every test. Conditional on survival, each projection prepares exactly the same initial state. The joint survival probability is \(P_i(\tau)^N\simeq[1-(T/Nt_Z)^2]^N\to1\) as \(N\to\infty\) for fixed total duration \(T\). This is a mathematically precise limiting model of the quantum Zeno effect. It is not obtained by simply looking at a stored record more often after a single final measurement; the successive physical interventions reset the conditional state.

With a resonantly coupled two-state Hamiltonian \(H=v\sigma_x\), the survival against a single interval is \(\cos^2(v\tau/\hbar)\). Choose \(vT/\hbar=\pi/2\); unobserved survival at \(T\) is zero. For ten equally spaced perfect tests the joint survival is \(\cos^{20}(\pi/20)=0.7805\). If the instrument has 2% independent loss per successful test, the recorded joint fraction falls to \(0.7805(0.98)^{10}=0.638\). A finite-duration detector may also change \(H\) or broaden spectral coupling rather than implement an instantaneous projection. The ideal repeated-measurement result gives a comparison baseline, while predicted observed counts must include the physical instrument and conditioning.

Derive the sweep-rate scale for a transition through resonance

For a chirped weak drive let its instantaneous detuning from one discrete transition change linearly, \(\Delta(t)=\dot\Delta\,t\), crossing zero at the middle of a long pulse. The phase accumulated in the first-order integral is \(\int^t\Delta(t')dt'=\dot\Delta\,t^2/2\), not \(\Delta(t)t\). For approximately constant coupling \(v\) around the crossing and sufficiently far endpoints the oscillatory Gaussian integral gives \(|c_f^{(1)}|^2\simeq2\pi|v|^2/(\hbar^2|\dot\Delta|)\). A slower passage accumulates a larger resonant amplitude, but this weak-coupling formula cannot exceed probability one. At large transfer the full two-level avoided-crossing dynamics replaces it.

For \(|v|/\hbar=2\pi\times200\) Hz and an ordinary-frequency sweep \(d(\Delta/2\pi)/dt=10^8\) Hz/s, the estimate is \(0.0158\). Changing only the sign of the sweep gives the same first-order probability in this symmetric idealisation, while the phase of the transition amplitude changes. Coherently driving a second passage can convert that phase difference into an observable interference pattern. Missing endpoints, asymmetric pulse shaping and additional nearby lines can spoil the simple symmetry. Comparing both sweep directions and checking that the result scales as inverse speed over a low-transfer range are concrete tests of the stationary-phase approximation instead of assuming every apparent line crossing signifies complete adiabatic transfer.

Separate a transition frequency from coherence loss

Vary detuning and shot-to-shot spread while reading both pulse quadratures. Determine whether a change of population comes from a shifted carrier or a fading phase.

Open the live experiment

Derive the spectral distinction between a phase-coherent pulse and stationary noise

A fluctuating perturbation is often characterised by a power spectral density rather than a prescribed pulse phase. Suppose \(V_{fi}(t)=d_{fi}F(t)\) with \(F\) a real classical, zero-mean stationary random process and the coupling weak enough for first-order amplitudes in each realisation. Averaging the modulus squared produces a double time integral involving the autocorrelation \(C_F(\tau)=\langle F(t+\tau)F(t)\rangle\):

\[\langle P_f(T)\rangle=\frac{|d_{fi}|^2}{\hbar^2}\int_{-T}^{T}(T-|\tau|)C_F(\tau)e^{i\omega_{fi}\tau}\,d\tau.\]

The factor \(T-|\tau|\) counts the length of pairs of times in \([0,T]\) separated by \(\tau\). If \(T\) is long compared with the correlation time and depletion remains weak, divide by \(T\) to find rate \(|d_{fi}|^2S_F(\omega_{fi})/\hbar^2\), where \(S_F(\omega)=\int_{-\infty}^\infty C_F(\tau)e^{i\omega\tau}d\tau\) is the two-sided angular-frequency spectrum. No sum over a continuum of final quantum states was involved here. A continuum of drive frequencies supplies a different physical route to an approximately linear transition probability of one resolved final state.

For exponentially correlated noise \(C_F(\tau)=F_{\rm rms}^2e^{-|\tau|/\tau_c}\), integration gives \(S_F(\omega)=2F_{\rm rms}^2\tau_c/[1+\omega^2\tau_c^2]\). Fix \(F_{\rm rms}\) and vary correlation time. At a nonzero fixed transition frequency, extremely rapid fluctuations have little integrated spectral weight there because their fixed variance is spread over a very broad band. Very slow fluctuations concentrate their weight near zero frequency. Differentiating the spectral expression gives a maximum excitation rate at \(\tau_c=1/|\omega_{fi}|\). This nonmonotonic dependence cannot be inferred just by quoting the field's total rms amplitude. Unit conventions are also critical: changing to ordinary-frequency spectral densities and a one-sided instrument display requires the corresponding Jacobian and factor of two.

A real classical stationary process has an even spectrum \(S_F(-\omega)=S_F(\omega)\), so this model drives upward and downward transitions equally per available source population when matrix elements are conjugate. A quantum environment at thermal equilibrium can have unequal positive and negative frequency unsymmetrised spectra. Its absorption and emission probabilities encode detailed balance. Thus an observed difference between calibrated upward and downward rates cannot generally be explained by merely drawing samples from a real classical noise spectrum of the same symmetrised power. Distinguishing a driven classical source from an equilibrium quantum bath requires precise statements about which correlations enter the transition probability.

Infer a fluctuation timescale from a rate ratio

Two transitions with equal coupling strengths see the same exponentially correlated classical field at ordinary frequencies \(f\) and \(2f\). Their measured weak transition rates obey \(\Gamma(2f)/\Gamma(f)=0.40\). Infer the dimensionless correlation frequency and, for \(f=50.0\) kHz, the correlation time. What feature of the acquisition would invalidate this inference?

Derive and check the result

Let \(x=(2\pi f\tau_c)^2\). The ratio is \((1+x)/(1+4x)=0.40\), giving \(0.60=0.60x\) and \(x=1\). The correlation time is \(1/(2\pi f)=3.18\,\mu\mathrm s\). At the lower frequency the noise spectrum lies at half its zero-frequency value; the higher line samples one fifth of that value. If the exposure time is comparable to or shorter than \(\tau_c\), the triangular window in the double integral still influences both rates and the infinite-time Lorentzian ratio is inadequate. Likewise unequal matrix elements or a finite observation band must be corrected separately. Measuring a third transition at another frequency supplies an independent test of the exponential-correlation model rather than simply determining its fitted timescale.

Tutorial · choose a pulse duration and check the perturbation

Measure a frequency width without confusing two line shapes

A rectangular pulse lasts 4.00 ms with resonant coupling \(|v|/\hbar=25.0\,\mathrm{s^{-1}}\). Find the line-centre probability, separation of the first zeros and full half-height width in hertz.

Work through the solution

At resonance first-order probability is \((25\times0.004)^2=0.0100\). The first zeros are 250 Hz on either side, giving a 500 Hz separation. The full half-height width is \(0.886/0.004=221.5\) Hz. A Lorentzian fit with its width interpreted as a spontaneous lifetime would mistake pulse broadening for decay. Doubling \(T\) while keeping coupling fixed is a diagnostic: the first-order peak would rise fourfold and the width halve. At large peak probability use the full two-level dynamics rather than extrapolating the quadratic indefinitely.

Verify the breakdown against exact two-level dynamics

Assume an isolated resonantly driven two-state system with rotating-frame Hamiltonian off-diagonal \(v\) and zero detuning. What replaces the first-order expression when \(T=\pi\hbar/(2|v|)\)? At which small pulse area is the fractional error approximately one percent?

Work through the solution

The resonant Hamiltonian squares to \(|v|^2I\), so the exact probability is \(\sin^2(|v|T/\hbar)\). At the stated pulse time it is unity while the first-order result is \((\pi/2)^2=2.47\), impossible as a probability. For pulse area \(a=|v|T/\hbar\), \(\sin^2a=a^2-a^4/3+\cdots\). The relative discrepancy is approximately \(a^2/3\), reaching 1% at \(a\simeq0.173\). The pulse can already make a measurable few-percent transition while first-order theory remains reasonably accurate. A dense continuum requires checking the total across all levels as well.

Infer an accessible density from the observed time dependence

At long-enough but weak-depletion times an ensemble loses 2.0% population in 5.0 ns. The squared box matrix element is calibrated as \(2.5\times10^{-17}\,\mathrm{eV^2}\). Infer \(\rho\), estimate the recurrence scale and test whether this interpretation is internally consistent.

Work through the solution

The slope estimate is \(4.0\times10^6\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}\). With \(\hbar=6.5821\times10^{-16}\,\mathrm{eV\,s}\) the inferred \(\rho=\Gamma\hbar/(2\pi|v|^2)=1.68\times10^7\,\mathrm{eV^{-1}}\). The corresponding spacing is \(5.97\times10^{-8}\) eV and recurrence scale \(h/\delta E\simeq69\) ns. A 5 ns observation fits within that scale, with about 14 spacings to the first zero. Two percent is compatible with weak depletion. Smoothness of the density and a single accessible channel still require independent spectral checks. Repeating the measurement at several durations reveals whether the inferred slope is stable.

Which part of a Bell correlation survives imperfect state preparation?

Computational quantum experiments

A source emits two spin-half systems in the singlet state. Two observers select measurement directions separately and record outcomes \(+1\) or \(-1\). Construct a Bell–CHSH statistic from four measurable correlations and determine how state preparation, angular alignment and finite sample size affect the prediction.

Relate a counted coincidence to an operator

Denote unit measurement directions by \(\mathbf a,\mathbf b\). Outcome projectors are \((1+s\,\boldsymbol\sigma\cdot\mathbf a)/2\) and \((1+t\,\boldsymbol\sigma\cdot\mathbf b)/2\), for \(s,t=\pm1\). The singlet has zero individual spin expectation and tensor \(\langle\sigma_i\otimes\sigma_j\rangle=-\delta_{ij}\). The diagonal entries follow because measurements along any common axis are exactly opposite and the state is rotationally invariant. Its joint probability is

\[p(s,t|\mathbf a,\mathbf b)=\tfrac14(1-st\,\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b),\]
\[E(\mathbf a,\mathbf b)=-\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b.\]

Summing over either outcome leaves one half for the other observer, independent of the remote angle. Covariance can change while the individual counting rates remain constant. At an angle of 60 degrees the probabilities of the two equal-outcome combinations sum to one quarter, and of opposite outcomes to three quarters. Their signed difference gives −0.5. The lab models spin-axis angles; linear polarisation analysers give a doubled angle in their effective two-state representation.

Derive a bound and choose a set of directions

If a trial had four predetermined local binary responses \(A,A',B,B'\), its combination \(AB+AB'+A'B-A'B'=A(B+B')+A'(B-B')\) would have magnitude two, since exactly one of the parentheses vanishes. Averaging over a common distribution of such assignments preserves the bound. The quantum prediction combines expectation values measured on different subensembles with independently chosen setting pairs. Use coplanar angles \(a=0,\ a'=\pi/2,\ b=\theta,\ b'=-\theta\).

\[S=E(a,b)+E(a,b')+E(a',b)-E(a',b')=-2(\cos\theta+\sin\theta).\]

At \(\theta=45^\circ\) its magnitude is \(2\sqrt2\). The derivative vanishes there, making small symmetric angular errors second order. If a fraction \(1-v\) of pairs is completely unpolarised, the density operator is \(v|\Psi^-\rangle\langle\Psi^-|+(1-v)I/4\). Every correlator gains a factor \(v\). The best attainable magnitude exceeds two only when \(v>1/\sqrt2\). This is a statement about this measurement family and this noise model. A pair source and a scalar contrast alone do not define an experimental protocol.

Assess one set of finite counts

At \(v=0.80\), \(\theta=45^\circ\) the expected magnitude is about 2.263. Each trial product is a binary variable with variance \(1-E^2\). For \(N\) independent pairs per setting, the sample correlation variance is \((1-E^2)/N\). Assuming independent subensembles, variance of the signed sum is the sum of the four variances. Here every squared correlation is 0.32. With 1000 trials per setting the approximate standard error of the statistic is \(\sqrt{4(1-0.32)/1000}=0.0522\). The excess 0.263 is roughly five standard errors under this idealised independent-trial model. It is not a substitute for a finite-sample hypothesis test that accounts for an actual protocol's setting choices, losses, timing and possible memory.

Change settings and preparation independently

Inspect the predicted absolute statistic and the local assignment bound. Find the angles for which the chosen source can exceed two. Then vary visibility at a fixed angle. Small individual spin polarisation drifts could alter the marginal counts; unequal channel efficiencies could change a coincidence-selected sample. Monitoring all four joint count tables and both single-observer marginal distributions allows more checks than retaining only their final sum.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial

At visibility 0.70, can increasing the number of trials eventually make the expected maximum exceed two?

Work through the solution

No. The largest mean value is \(2\sqrt2(0.70)=1.980\). More trials narrow the sampling distribution around that value. Changing the preparation or measurement family addresses a different question.

What is the predicted fractional loss at an angle \(\delta\) away from the optimum, and how large is it at five degrees?

Work through the solution

Write \(\cos\theta+\sin\theta=\sqrt2\cos(\theta-\pi/4)\). The fractional loss is \(1-\cos\delta\), about \(0.00381\) at five degrees. Asymmetric offsets on different analyser axes need not enter the same way.

A paper gives one large value of \(|S|\) but discards all pairs for which one detector was silent. Can the ideal bound derived above automatically be applied to these retained counts?

Work through the solution

The derivation averaged all setting pairs over a common underlying ensemble. Setting- or state-dependent retention can alter those ensembles. One needs the trial definition, data handling and efficiency assumptions to interpret the reported statistic.

For state preparation and independent measurement on tensor factors, revisit the composite-state chapter.

Recover a transition frequency from two separated pulses

Computational quantum experiments

A two-level system is driven by two short pulses with a free evolution interval between them. Determine why a population measurement carries phase information. Use a scan at several delays to separate a constant detuning, intrinsic coherence loss and trial-to-trial frequency variation.

Follow the amplitudes through both pulses

Use basis \(|g\rangle,|e\rangle\). Let the first pulse prepare \((|g\rangle+|e\rangle)/\sqrt2\) in the frame of the applied oscillator. A detuning \(\Delta\) in radians per second accumulates a relative phase \(\Delta\tau\) in free time \(\tau\). Immediately before the second pulse each state has population one half; measuring population here cannot reveal detuning. Choose a second pulse equivalent to projecting onto \((|g\rangle+e^{-i\phi}|e\rangle)/\sqrt2\). Then one output population is

\[P(\tau,\phi)=\tfrac12[1+\cos(\Delta\tau-\phi)].\]

The sign of the phase follows our definitions of detuning and the reference pulse. Shifting the reference phase makes it possible to check that sign. A measurement using only \(\phi=0\) cannot distinguish \(\Delta\) from \(-\Delta\). Coherence is the off-diagonal density-matrix element that the second pulse converts into an observable population difference.

Derive two envelopes with different origins

A homogeneous decay time \(T_2\) multiplies the off-diagonal element by \(e^{-\tau/T_2}\). If instead the detuning of each experimental shot is constant during that shot and drawn from a Gaussian distribution of mean \(\bar\Delta\) and standard deviation \(\sigma_\Delta\), average the complex phase, not its absolute value. Completing the square in the Gaussian integral gives \(\langle e^{i\Delta\tau}\rangle=e^{i\bar\Delta\tau}e^{-\sigma_\Delta^2\tau^2/2}\). With both independent mechanisms the measured contrast is \(C(\tau)=\exp[-\tau/T_2-\sigma_\Delta^2\tau^2/2]\). One mechanism gives an initial logarithmic slope, the other adds curvature. A single low contrast measured at just one delay cannot establish which one caused it.

Infer parameters from quadratures and a frequency scan

Record the \(\phi=0,\pi/2\) outputs, then form \(X=2P_0-1=C\cos(\bar\Delta\tau)\) and \(Y=2P_{\pi/2}-1=C\sin(\bar\Delta\tau)\). Their length estimates contrast and their angle estimates accumulated phase modulo \(2\pi\). Suppose at \(0.50\,\mathrm{ms}\) the readings are 0.500 and 0.850. Then \(X=0,\ Y=0.700\), and the phase is \(\pi/2\) modulo \(2\pi\). With an independent bound \(|\bar\Delta/2\pi|<1\,\mathrm{kHz}\), the implied cyclic detuning is \(+500\,\mathrm{Hz}\). Longer delays resolve fine frequency differences at the cost of phase ambiguity and declining contrast. Use a shorter-delay record to unwrap a longer one.

At a contrast of 0.700 the relation \(-\ln(0.700)=\tau/T_2+\sigma_\Delta^2\tau^2/2\) supplies one equation for two unknowns. Several delays, with independent error bars, are needed to identify the linear and quadratic terms. At very low signal the length \(\sqrt{X^2+Y^2}\) has a positive noise bias. Fitting the two directly measured quadratures can be preferable to fitting the length.

Explore two mechanisms

The graph holds the homogeneous \(T_2\) at 2 ms. It lets you set signed cyclic detuning and shot-to-shot cyclic frequency spread. Compare both quadrature populations with the upper contrast envelope. First reverse the sign of the detuning, then increase the spread without changing the mean. Which effect interchanges peaks of one quadrature and which damps both?

Open the live experiment

Tutorial

What is the fringe spacing in a scan of cyclic detuning at fixed delay of 0.80 ms?

Work through the solution

One full phase cycle needs \(\delta f\,\tau=1\). The spacing is \(1/0.00080=1250\,\mathrm{Hz}\). The angular-frequency spacing is \(2\pi\) times larger.

A fixed delay gives \(P_0=P_{\pi/2}=0.5\). Does that show a phase of 45 degrees?

Work through the solution

Both centred quadratures are zero, so the contrast is unresolved and the angle is undefined. A phase of 45 degrees with finite contrast gives equal, nonzero centred quadratures.

Explain how a refocusing pulse halfway through the evolution helps identify quasistatic detuning noise.

Work through the solution

The pulse exchanges the phase-accumulating states, reversing phase winding for a detuning that stays constant through both halves. Random variations during the interval need not cancel. Recovering contrast in the refocused sequence supports a slowly varying contribution to the original envelope.

Compare a continuous drive in the transition chapter with the role of the second pulse here.

Mechanical and thermal effects of light

Calculate how a beam can translate a bead, couple resonances or exchange energy with a moving boundary. Test each proposal against a measured signal.

  1. Optical trapping and Brownian calibration of a dielectric bead
  2. Coupled optical and mechanical resonances
  3. Thermal radiance, étendue and detector calibration
  4. Moving-mirror Doppler metrology
  5. Where momentum waits between source and receiver
  6. Why a small transparent sphere scatters light
  7. From wavefronts to rays, focusing and caustics

Optical trapping and Brownian calibration of a dielectric bead

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

An image of a bead remaining near a focus does not establish the strength of its trap. Derive the force pulling a small dielectric sphere into a bright region, compare it with the forward force from scattering and learn what a motion record can tell you about stiffness, drag and temperature. The model here uses an inert dielectric sphere in a homogeneous Newtonian liquid.

From two dielectric boundaries to the energy of an induced dipole

A sphere of radius \(a\) and relative permittivity \(\epsilon_b\) sits in a liquid of relative permittivity \(\epsilon_m\). Assuming the sphere is small compared with a wavelength in the liquid, solve its local electric problem in an almost uniform applied field \(E\). Rotational symmetry about the field admits an interior potential proportional to \(r\cos\theta\). The exterior consists of the imposed potential \(-Er\cos\theta\) and an induced dipolar term proportional to \(\cos\theta/r^2\). Equality of potential and of normal displacement at the surface gives the local-field and dipole factors

\[E_{\rm in}=\frac{3\epsilon_m}{\epsilon_b+2\epsilon_m}E,\]
\[p=\alpha E,\]
\[\alpha=4\pi\epsilon_0\epsilon_m a^3\frac{\epsilon_b-\epsilon_m}{\epsilon_b+2\epsilon_m}.\]

The field doing the polarising work is the external field in the host at the particle position before inserting it. To turn that work into a potential, build up the dipole from zero rather than assuming a permanent dipole moment. Quasistatic assembly gives \(-\int_0^E\alpha E'\,dE'=-\alpha E^2/2\). For a harmonic peak field average the square over an optical period. In a nearly lossless liquid of refractive index \(n_m\), the peak field is related to travelling-wave intensity by \(I=n_m\epsilon_0c|E|^2/2\). The resulting conservative force is

\[U=-\frac{\operatorname{Re}\alpha}{4}|E|^2=-\frac{\operatorname{Re}\alpha}{2n_m\epsilon_0 c}I,\]
\[\mathbf F_{\rm grad}=-\nabla U .\]

The two factors of two have different origins: induced-dipole assembly and time averaging of a sinusoid. Using a permanent-dipole potential with a peak field would miss both. A bead of higher refractive index than the liquid has positive real polarizability and is attracted to increasing intensity. A lower-index inclusion reverses this local tendency. For large particles, strong absorption or rapidly structured fields, a dipole with real static polarizability no longer specifies the force; a field or multipole calculation of the complete momentum balance is required.

Investigate the small-sphere dipole response and its dependence on particle size

Compute a transverse stiffness and test how local it is

Assume the beam near its focal plane has intensity \(I(x)=I_0e^{-2x^2/w^2}\), with power \(P\), waist \(w\) defined at \(1/e^2\) intensity and \(I_0=2P/(\pi w^2)\). Put \(U_0=\operatorname{Re}\alpha I_0/(2n_m\epsilon_0c)>0\). The potential measured above its minimum is \(U_0[1-e^{-2x^2/w^2}]\). Expanding to second order and equating its leading term to \(\kappa_xx^2/2\) gives \(\kappa_x=4U_0/w^2\). Stiffness therefore scales linearly with power, as the third power of sphere radius and as the inverse fourth power of waist at fixed indices. Each scaling applies within the stated approximations.

For a 40 nm radius bead with \(n_b=1.59\), take \(n_m=1.33\), \(w=0.65\) micrometres, vacuum wavelength 1064 nm, \(P=90\) mW and \(T=298\) K. The index contrast factor is 0.1252 and the waist intensity is \(1.36\times10^{11}\) watts per square metre. The predicted depth \(3.03\times10^{-20}\) J is 7.36 times the thermal energy. The lateral stiffness is \(2.87\times10^{-7}\) N/m. A displacement of 50 nm then requires approximately 14.3 fN of transverse balancing force. At a displacement of half a waist, the exact restoring force is the harmonic estimate multiplied by \(e^{-1/2}=0.607\); a Hookean calibration cannot be extrapolated that far.

Open the live experiment

Use the two curves to identify the interval in which the local quadratic energy is within ten percent of the Gaussian prediction. First lower power at fixed waist. Both curves shrink together in energy units while the harmonic thermal displacement grows. Next increase bead radius at fixed power and watch the well deepen, but also evaluate the size parameter \(2\pi n_ma/\lambda_0\). It is 0.314 at the default and grows linearly with radius. Stronger trapping predicted by this simplified model does not establish the accuracy of the dipole approximation at arbitrary sizes.

Compare axial restoring force with radiation pressure

An induced dipole reradiates a portion of the incident field. In a uniform host use the Rayleigh scattering cross section \(\sigma_s=(8\pi/3)k_m^4a^6|(\epsilon_b-\epsilon_m)/(\epsilon_b+2\epsilon_m)|^2\). In a common travelling-wave momentum-flux model for a bead in a stationary nondispersive liquid the axial scattering force is approximately \(F_s=n_m I\sigma_s/c\); absorption would add a further contribution. This estimate uses the total scattering cross section because the leading dipole pattern has vanishing mean forward cosine. For a larger forward-scattering sphere a transport cross section weights the deflected momentum instead.

In the default case the cross section is roughly \(2.05\times10^{-18}\) square metres. The forward force at the waist is about 1.23 fN. A Gaussian axial profile has \(I(0,z)=I_0/[1+(z/z_R)^2]\) with \(z_R=\pi n_mw^2/\lambda_0=1.66\) micrometres. Its local axial stiffness \(2U_0/z_R^2=2.20\times10^{-8}\) N/m is much smaller than its transverse stiffness. Balancing the constant forward force with that local stiffness predicts an equilibrium about 56 nm downstream of the waist. The small displacement relative to the Rayleigh length supports the linearisation.

Both the axial stiffness and forward scattering force scale with power in this simple model. A power sweep can thus increase confinement while barely shifting the equilibrium offset, a useful discriminating prediction. At fixed focus the offset grows rapidly with bead size because the scattering force scales with the sixth power and the gradient force with the third power. A stable lateral position alone does not establish stable three-dimensional capture. Check the axial restoring slope at a force zero and verify that thermal escape and fluid flow are negligible over the acquisition interval.

Extract stiffness from fluctuations or from a relaxation time

Near a stable point in one Cartesian direction, let \(x\) denote displacement from the mean and \(\zeta\) the viscous drag. At sufficiently low frequencies the inertial term is negligible and the overdamped Langevin equation is \(\zeta\dot x=-\kappa x+\xi\). At equilibrium the fluctuating force has zero mean and white covariance \(2k_BT\zeta\delta(t-t')\) over the frequencies considered. The deterministic relaxation time is \(\tau=\zeta/\kappa\). Multiplying the equation at positive delay by \(x(0)\) and averaging removes the uncorrelated later noise and gives the decaying covariance. Equipartition sets its initial value.

\[C(t)=\frac{k_BT}{\kappa}e^{-|t|/\tau},\]
\[f_c=\frac{\kappa}{2\pi\zeta},\]
\[S_x^{(1)}(f)=\frac{4k_BT\zeta}{\kappa^2+(2\pi f\zeta)^2},\]
\[f\ge0 .\]

The stated spectrum is one-sided in ordinary frequency. Its integral from zero to infinity equals \(C(0)\); doubling another time would double the variance. In an unbounded fluid with viscosity 1.00 mPa s, Stokes drag is \(6\pi\eta a=7.54\times10^{-10}\) N s/m. The default predicts \(f_c=60.5\) Hz and rms displacement 120 nm. The latter is roughly 0.18 waist, so the outer part of a long thermal trajectory may sample appreciable anharmonicity. A finite-depth well in an unbounded bath does not have a globally normalisable trapped equilibrium. These local equilibrium predictions apply to a sufficiently long-lived, locally sampled trapped state.

If a voltage detector gives \(V=gx\), the spectral corner and normalised autocorrelation time do not depend on the displacement gain \(g\). They determine only \(\kappa/\zeta\). Equipartition using a calibrated spatial variance gives \(\kappa\) independently of drag, provided mechanical fluctuations are in equilibrium and sufficiently resolved. If the viscosity or nearby-wall drag is uncertain, combining a known stage oscillation with the Brownian spectrum can determine further calibration quantities. Keep a stage-driven coherent peak out of the noise integral used for equipartition.

Account for exposure, sample size and unobserved motion

A camera typically reports the position averaged during an exposure of duration \(t_e\). Its variance is the double integral of the covariance over the exposure interval divided by \(t_e^2\). Evaluating the exponential local model and introducing \(u=t_e/\tau\) gives a multiplicative factor \(2(u-1+e^{-u})/u^2\). At \(u=1\) the reported physical-motion variance is only 0.736 of the instantaneous variance. Interpreting it as the full variance would overestimate stiffness by approximately 36 percent. Independent pixel-localisation noise adds additional variance with the opposite sign of bias.

Estimate that localisation noise with an effectively immobilised reference bead at a comparable brightness. Test the averaging correction by changing exposure duration at constant sampling frequency where possible. A camera that reports overlapping exposures has correlated measurement errors and needs a different covariance model. A long series of closely spaced frames also contains fewer independent thermal configurations than its frame count suggests. Compare estimates across several blocks substantially longer than the relaxation time; residual low-frequency drift can dominate even when high-frequency position precision looks excellent.

A separate check uses a known uniform liquid flow. At a steady small displacement \(x_s\), force balance gives \(\kappa x_s=\zeta u_{\rm flow}\). For the default drag and stiffness a flow of 10 micrometres per second gives an offset of 26.3 nm. Repeat with reversed flow and use half the displacement difference to cancel an unknown zero-force position. Near a wall, the local flow at the bead and wall-corrected drag may differ from a stage velocity and the unbounded Stokes value. Those effects alter the mechanical calibration even if the optical field is unchanged.

A known stage oscillation calibrates position in a fluid

Brownian fluctuations alone determine the product of several unknown calibration parameters. A weak sinusoidal translation of the sample cell adds a known mechanical signal at a chosen frequency. Work in laboratory coordinates with a fixed optical trap and with fluid moving uniformly at velocity \(u(t)\) near the bead, well away from walls in the idealised model. The drag is now \(-\zeta(\dot x-u)\). Superposing a stage displacement \(A\cos\omega t\) on thermal fluctuations gives a deterministic response whose complex amplitude satisfies \((\kappa-i\omega\zeta)\widetilde x=-i\omega\zeta A\) in the negative-exponential convention. Therefore its amplitude ratio is

\[\frac{|\widetilde x|}{A}=\frac{\omega\tau}{\sqrt{1+\omega^2\tau^2}} .\]

At the trap corner the bead displacement amplitude is the known stage amplitude divided by the square root of two. A calibrated 80.0 nm stage oscillation there produces a predicted bead amplitude of 56.6 nm. If a position-sensitive receiver records a coherent peak amplitude of 0.170 V, its displacement gain is 3.01 mV/nm. At very low drive frequencies the fluid velocity is small for fixed stage amplitude and the trap holds the bead nearly stationary. At high enough frequency the bead follows the fluid displacement within the overdamped model, but finite hydrodynamic, optical and electronic bandwidths eventually matter.

After determining the gain, convert the surrounding voltage spectrum into spatial units and use the area of the stochastic fluctuation spectrum to find stiffness. Exclude the calibrated line and account for the finite spectral averaging window; a narrow drive that leaks into neighbouring bins can inflate the inferred thermal variance. Dividing measured stiffness by the independently determined corner rate then gives drag, which can be compared with the known viscosity and sphere radius. This avoids assuming free-space Stokes drag in the first step of a near-wall measurement.

There are two limiting systematic effects that a good fit to a single drive frequency cannot resolve. A frequency-dependent stage calibration changes the actual fluid displacement, and motion of the optics relative to the cell generates a direct detector background even with the bead immobilised. Repeat the drive above and below the corner, compare coherent phase as well as amplitude and acquire a trapped and immobilised reference. Correct conversion should explain the entire complex response with one spatial gain and one corner time. A single calibration number obtained at one frequency can hide a phase lag in the stage controller or in the optical position channel.

One last cross-check reverses the stage drive phase by half a period. The coherent bead response must reverse, while thermal background power remains unchanged. A component that fails this reversal could come from ambient vibration unrelated to the stage. Averaging the two traces with and without a sign change separates these contributions before either is used in an optical-force calibration.

Tutorial · Separate a unit-conversion error from excess noise

In a single-axis trap at 298 K a calibrated detector reports a variance of 2500 square nanometres after removal of localisation noise and exposure correction. An independent spectral fit gives a corner of 175 Hz. Infer stiffness, drag and the equivalent unbounded hydrodynamic radius for a liquid of viscosity 1.00 mPa s. Compare against a nominal 80 nm radius bead.

Infer the three quantities and test their consistency

A nanometre squared is \(10^{-18}\) square metres, so the variance is \(2.50\times10^{-15}\) square metres. Equipartition gives \(1.65\times10^{-6}\) N/m. Dividing by \(2\pi f_c\) gives drag \(1.50\times10^{-9}\) N s/m and radius \(\zeta/(6\pi\eta)=79.5\) nm. The agreement supports the local overdamped description under the assumed temperature and viscosity. Forgetting the factor of \(2\pi\) would predict a radius roughly six times larger and could be mistaken for a huge near-wall correction.

Now imagine that additional uncorrelated read noise had been left in the variance. This raises apparent variance, lowers the equipartition stiffness and would lower the inferred drag at the same fitted corner. Nearby-wall drag for a given radius instead normally raises hydrodynamic resistance for parallel translation. Their effects on the inferred radius thus differ in sign under the stated assumptions. Varying height and independently calibrating the measurement noise are appropriate diagnostics before attributing a discrepancy to bead dimensions.

Tutorial · Test a force-law inference with power and concentration controls

At fixed bead radius a trap is operated at two powers differing by a factor of two. Measured corner frequencies increase from 42 to 84 Hz, but rms displacement falls only from 110 to 87 nm. What does the harmonic model with fixed drag and temperature predict? Estimate a power-independent additive position-noise variance that could reconcile the two readings.

Use two variances to separate scale and offset

The doubled corner gives doubled stiffness if drag is unchanged. Physical thermal variance should halve. The predicted rms at higher power would be 77.8 nm if the lower-power value were entirely physical. Let the low-power measured variance be \(v+b\) and the high-power one \(v/2+b\), where \(b\) is a constant additive noise variance. Solving from 12,100 and 7,569 square nanometres gives \(v=9062\) and \(b=3038\) square nanometres, or approximately 55.1 nm rms noise.

This is a large inferred detector noise and demands an independent immobilised reference check. Power-dependent heating of the liquid changes temperature and viscosity, so an unchanged product inferred from only two quantities cannot prove that heating is absent. Test several powers, reverse their order to reveal slow drift and vary bead concentration to exclude overlapping particles in the tracking window. The inferred additive term should remain constant across those controls if it comes from the specified detector model.

Tolić-Nørrelykke and colleagues · position and force calibration using thermal and driven measurements

Coupled optical and mechanical resonances

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A vibrating mirror can imprint its position on an optical field, while the stored light pushes back on the mirror. An apparent pair of peaks can indicate two hybrid modes, interference at a readout port or two unrelated resonances. Starting from energy in a moving cavity, derive the coupled response and learn which measurements establish a coupling strength and its uncertainty.

From a moving boundary to a force per photon

Take a nearly empty Fabry–Pérot cavity of optical length \(L\) with one moving end. A longitudinal resonance has \(\omega_c=m\pi c/L\) for integer \(m\), neglecting frequency-dependent mirror phase. For a small extension \(x\), \(\delta\omega_c=(\partial\omega_c/\partial x)x\) with signed slope \(G_x=-\omega_c/L\). At fixed photon number \(n\) the optical energy is \(n\hbar\omega_c(x)\). Its derivative gives the radiation-pressure force on the coordinate. For this mirror \(F=-n\hbar G_x>0\) pushes outward. The derivative must be at fixed photon number. Differentiating a driven steady-state photon number as though it were a closed-system energy would incorrectly include work exchanged with the pump.

For a 10 mm cavity resonant near 1064 nm the frequency slope magnitude in ordinary frequency is about 28.2 MHz per nanometre. A static shift of 10 pm therefore changes the optical resonance by approximately 282 kHz. One photon exerts a mean force \(\hbar\omega_c/L\simeq1.87\times10^{-17}\) N. An equivalent classical derivation counts a momentum change twice per normal reflection for each relevant round trip. The two descriptions yield the same mean force. The mirror displacement is the properly normalised mode coordinate; in a flexural device the optically sampled position and the total physical mass are generally different from centre-of-mass displacement and mass.

Introduce a single mechanical eigenmode with effective mass \(m_{\rm eff}\), angular frequency \(\Omega_m\) and displacement scale \(x_{\rm zpf}=\sqrt{\hbar/(2m_{\rm eff}\Omega_m)}\). The single-photon coupling magnitude is \(g_0=|G_x|x_{\rm zpf}\). This normalisation is convenient even when the observed vibrations are entirely classical. For \(m_{\rm eff}=10\) ng and \(\Omega_m/(2\pi)=1.0\) MHz, \(x_{\rm zpf}=9.16\times10^{-16}\) m and \(g_0/(2\pi)=25.8\) Hz for the stated optical geometry. The small displacement scale explains why a coherent pump is normally used to make the interaction measurable.

How a laser couples frequencies that are otherwise far apart

An optical carrier oscillates at hundreds of terahertz, whereas the mirror may vibrate at only megahertz. Direct comparison of these two bare frequencies would never suggest a resonant exchange. A strong coherent pump supplies a phase reference and converts a mechanical displacement into optical sidebands. Move into a frame rotating at the pump frequency and write the cavity amplitude as its large steady part plus a small perturbation. The leading frequency-shift interaction is proportional to photon number times displacement. Expanding the intensity around the large pump keeps terms linear in both the probe field and displacement. The resulting coupling is \(G=g_0\sqrt{n_c}\), where \(n_c\) is mean intracavity pump photon number.

Tune the pump about one mechanical frequency below the optical cavity resonance. In the resolved-sideband and weak-fluctuation limit a near-resonant process moves one excitation between the mechanical mode and an upper optical sideband. The rapidly rotating opposite-sideband terms average out. Now the two coupled frequencies are an optical sideband offset and the mechanical frequency, measured relative to a common rotating reference. The large carrier frequency has already been accounted for by the pump. The pump phase can be absorbed into the mode definitions, so \(G\) may be chosen positive for this isolated pair.

If instead the pump is placed near the upper sideband, the surviving interaction creates or annihilates excitations in pairs. The passive exchange equations below cease to apply and strong pumping can produce amplification or instability. A bare two-oscillator machine driven mechanically near a shared natural frequency often has analogous exchange equations without a carrier pump. Identical matrices can describe different energy supplies; identify the drive and rotating reference before assigning a literal photon-to-phonon energy conversion.

Derive complex poles and the effect of unequal damping

Let \(a,b\) be small energy-normalised optical and mechanical complex amplitudes. In a frame where the uncoupled optical sideband has zero frequency, let the mechanical detuning be \(\delta\). Let \(\kappa,\Gamma\) denote energy linewidths in angular frequency, so amplitude decay rates are half these values. With an optical probe \(s\) at offset \(\omega\), the linear system is

\[\begin{pmatrix}\kappa/2-i\omega&iG\\ iG&\Gamma/2-i(\omega-\delta)\end{pmatrix}\binom{a}{b}=\binom{s}{0}.\]

Zeros of the determinant give autonomous complex mode frequencies. In the lossless case the eigenvalues are \(\delta/2\pm\sqrt{G^2+(\delta/2)^2}\). Far off resonance each branch approaches a bare mode. At zero detuning the two eigenvectors have equal optical and mechanical weights and the splitting is \(2G\). The eigenvectors rotate continuously through the avoided crossing, an important discriminator from two uncoupled lines simply passing through one another.

At zero detuning with losses, completing the square in the determinant gives

\[\omega_\pm=-i(\kappa+\Gamma)/4\ \pm\sqrt{G^2-(\kappa-\Gamma)^2/16}.\]

The real parts separate once \(G>|\kappa-\Gamma|/4\). The shared imaginary part above that threshold corresponds to a hybrid energy linewidth \((\kappa+\Gamma)/2\). Resolving two intensity features on a given instrument is a stronger, readout-dependent condition. The dimensionless cooperativity \(C=4G^2/(\kappa\Gamma)\) is yet another measure, comparing interaction against the product of dissipation scales. A value greater than one does not automatically prove two resolved normal-mode poles in a device with very unequal linewidths.

Use frequency numbers divided by \(2\pi\) consistently from here for examples. With optical linewidth 240 kHz, mechanical linewidth 40 kHz and coupling 160 kHz, the real pole separation is \(2\sqrt{160^2-50^2}=304\) kHz. Both hybrid energy linewidths are 140 kHz, and cooperativity is 10.7. If coupling falls to 45 kHz, cooperativity is still about 0.844 and no real zero-detuning pole separation appears. At 50 kHz the square root reaches zero. Near that threshold even a small linewidth or detuning calibration error has a large effect on a coupling estimate inferred from a single splitting.

Compute what an optical or mechanical receiver actually sees

Invert the matrix. With \(D=(\kappa/2-i\omega)[\Gamma/2-i(\omega-\delta)]+G^2\), the small amplitudes are \(a=s[\Gamma/2-i(\omega-\delta)]/D\) and \(b=-iGs/D\). The poles are shared. Their numerators are different, so the two channels can show different apparent maxima. A port in reflection also has a directly reflected field that interferes with the leaking intracavity amplitude. Its intensity can display a narrow dip or asymmetric feature without a matching resonance-height pattern inside the cavity. A legitimate forward model must describe the measured port.

Open the live experiment

The graph plots the internal squared amplitudes relative to the bare on-resonance optical probe energy. Set the mechanical detuning to zero and reduce the coupling through 50 kHz. Compare the optical dip with the predicted real pole separation. Then set a substantial positive detuning and follow the transfer of mechanical response onto one branch. As a control set the coupling exactly to zero. The optical curve recovers the bare cavity Lorentzian and the mechanical probe-induced response vanishes. A thermally excited mechanical background, absent from this driven calculation, could remain in a real detector.

At zero detuning and zero probe offset, \(a/a_{\rm bare}=1/(1+C)\). For the default parameters the internal optical probe intensity at the centre is only \(1/(1+10.67)^2=0.00735\) of the bare peak. This deep feature alone still does not establish the pole spacing. An incoherent background, a frequency-dependent detector transfer or coherent feedthrough can alter peak and dip positions. Fit complex quadratures across a detuning series when available, and verify bare linewidths by independent ringdown or far-detuned spectra.

Check how energy linewidth and amplitude ringdown are related for one isolated resonance

Watch energy exchange in time and identify the linewidth convention

In an ideal equal-linewidth system starting with optical perturbation \(a(0)=a_0\), \(b(0)=0\) at zero detuning, change to symmetric and antisymmetric modes. Recombination gives \(a(t)=a_0e^{-\kappa t/2}\cos Gt\) and \(b(t)=-ia_0e^{-\kappa t/2}\sin Gt\). Their normalised total energy decays as \(e^{-\kappa t}\), while energy moves back and forth between optical sideband and vibration. The first full transfer occurs at \(t=\pi/(2G)\) if the rotating-wave approximation holds. For \(G/(2\pi)=100\) kHz, this is 2.50 microseconds. With a shared energy linewidth of 20 kHz in ordinary frequency the surviving energy fraction then is \(e^{-2\pi(20\,{\rm kHz})(2.50\,\mu{\rm s})}=0.730\).

A gated excitation shorter than the exchange period but long enough to avoid unwanted modes can prepare an approximately optical initial perturbation. Independent detection of an optical revival and a delayed mechanical response tests coherent exchange more strongly than the presence of two peaks alone. Comparing times of maxima directly with one half the reciprocal measured optical linewidth misidentifies the exchange physics. Check the decay of the sum of appropriately calibrated energies and the relative phase of the exchange. Electrical or optical feedthrough can produce a prompt apparent response with no mechanical build-up.

Infer a coupling with propagated measurement errors

When the two hybrid pole frequencies have been obtained from a justified fit and zero bare detuning is established, the coupling follows from the measured real separation \(S\):

\[G=\sqrt{S^2/4+(\kappa-\Gamma)^2/16}.\]
