All quantities here can be expressed in the same ordinary-frequency units. If \(S=300\pm8\) kHz, \(\kappa=240\pm10\) kHz and \(\Gamma=40\pm4\) kHz, the estimate is 158.1 kHz. Independent small errors give \(\partial G/\partial S=S/(4G)=0.474\) and \(\partial G/\partial\kappa=-\partial G/\partial\Gamma=(\kappa-\Gamma)/(16G)=0.0791\). Combining in quadrature gives about 3.9 kHz uncertainty. A common frequency calibration can correlate all three inputs, and a fitted detuning and splitting from one scan may also be correlated. Use their covariance rather than multiplying independent error bars blindly.

A second inference varies intracavity pump power. Since \(G^2=g_0^2 n_c\), plot the squared fitted coupling against independently calibrated \(n_c\). A nonzero intercept could indicate an additional direct coupling, baseline fitting bias or a mode incorrectly assigned at low power. The linewidths themselves can vary with pump power because of heating or backaction, so fit or measure them across the scan. Incident optical power is not identical to stored photon number; cavity coupling, detuning and loss determine their relation.

A measured frequency shift and linewidth change reveal optical feedback

The same instrument can be operated below the resolved splitting regime and read out a dispersive shift of the mechanical response. Algebraically eliminate the optical amplitude in the two-mode equations while driving the mechanical mode weakly. Its inverse susceptibility acquires a self-energy contribution proportional to the optical susceptibility. Let \(x\) be probe frequency measured from the bare optical sideband and use the same linewidth convention as above. In the mechanical denominator the additional term is

\[\Sigma(x)=\frac{G^2}{\kappa/2-ix}=\frac{G^2(\kappa/2+ix)}{(\kappa/2)^2+x^2}.\]

Its real part adds to the amplitude damping, while its imaginary part shifts the resonant condition. At very weak coupling one can evaluate it at the bare mechanical frequency \(x=\delta\), because variation across the narrow mechanical peak is small. Near an avoided crossing or when the mechanical peak samples a large change in optical susceptibility, solve the coupled poles directly; simply adding a linewidth and shift then neglects the very hybridisation being tested.

In ordinary-frequency units choose \(G=10\) kHz, optical linewidth 200 kHz, mechanical linewidth 2 kHz and detuning \(+100\) kHz. At that offset the optical amplitude half-width is 100 kHz, giving \(\operatorname{Re}\Sigma=0.50\) kHz and \(\operatorname{Im}\Sigma=0.50\) kHz. The full mechanical linewidth increases from 2.0 to approximately 3.0 kHz. Its centre moves upwards by about 0.50 kHz with the adopted matrix signs. Moving to minus 100 kHz reverses the shift and preserves the added damping in this symmetric model. At zero detuning the predicted shift vanishes but the added linewidth is largest.

A scan on both sides is more discriminating than measuring broadening alone. A background rise in mechanical temperature changes integrated thermal noise and may change intrinsic loss, but need not reproduce this odd shift and even broadening pattern. Measure intrinsic linewidth far from the optical response and subtract that baseline. An unknown constant frequency offset can be removed with the two symmetric detunings, but an optical power dependence of cavity frequency can move the actual symmetry centre. Monitor the optical resonance during the scan.

The effective susceptibility describes classical feedback through the delayed optical field. It does not by itself specify the final mechanical temperature. A bath occupation or noise spectrum is also required to convert a damping change into an energy distribution. A driven response peak that broadens and lowers in amplitude is evidence for a changed susceptibility; thermal cooling additionally requires a calibrated measurement of the fluctuating mechanical displacement and its optical transduction.

Tutorial · A high cooperativity without resolved modes

A device has ordinary optical linewidth 1.00 MHz, mechanical linewidth 1.0 kHz and pump-enhanced coupling 30 kHz. Calculate cooperativity, compare the coupling with the real-splitting threshold and estimate the relative intracavity probe intensity at the central frequency. What observation would distinguish a narrow interference window from two separated poles?

Evaluate the three criteria independently

In kHz units the cooperativity is \(4(30)^2/(1000\cdot1)=3.6\). The real-splitting threshold is \(999/4=249.75\) kHz, far above the actual coupling. Yet the zero-detuning centre intensity is \(1/(1+3.6)^2=0.0473\) relative to the bare optical peak. A narrow, deep structured feature is possible with no separated real pole frequencies in this model. The linewidth contrast permits mechanical response to interfere strongly within a broad optical susceptibility.

Acquire both amplitude and phase while scanning the absolute mechanical detuning and compare a coupled complex-response model with independently measured bare parameters. A pair of well-resolved poles should trace two consistent trajectories in both observables and can generate underdamped exchange in the time domain. A single optical intensity scan containing two flanks around a dip is insufficient to make that inference.

Tutorial · Diagnose an erroneous estimate of the single-photon coupling

An independent linewidth analysis gives pump-enhanced couplings 80 and 160 kHz at photon numbers \(4.0\times10^6\) and \(1.6\times10^7\). Estimate \(g_0/(2\pi)\) and predict the coupling at \(9.0\times10^6\) photons. A calibration revision doubles all inferred photon numbers while leaving the measured spectra unchanged. Which conclusions move?

Use coherent amplitude scaling and separate calibration from spectroscopy

Divide the respective couplings in ordinary frequency by the square roots of their photon numbers. Both give 40 Hz and the intermediate prediction is 120 kHz. Doubling all inferred photon numbers lowers the inferred single-photon coupling by \(1/\sqrt2\), to 28.3 Hz. The observed enhanced couplings, pole locations and cooperativities computed from measured linewidths stay the same. A prediction labelled by fixed physical photon number changes; a prediction at a repeatable laser setting can still be calibrated empirically.

To test the absolute calibration, compare the inferred single-photon value with the independent cavity frequency slope times the mechanical zero-point displacement. This requires an effective mass appropriate to the measured displacement and the actual cavity optical overlap. An incorrect choice of mechanical mode normalisation can mimic an error in stored photon number. Agreement across a power ratio tests the square-root scaling but cannot by itself determine the absolute optical or mechanical calibration.

Aspelmeyer, Kippenberg and Marquardt · cavity optomechanics, linearisation and strong coupling

Thermal radiance, étendue and detector calibration

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A square millimetre of a heated object emits thermal radiation into an entire hemisphere. A detector sees only the rays accepted by its optics and only wavelengths to which it responds. Derive the equilibrium spectrum, follow it through an optical collection system and infer what temperature or emissivity a calibrated reading can actually determine.

Count optical states in a large equilibrium cavity

Take a cubic airless cavity of volume \(V\) in thermal equilibrium at temperature \(T\). For its bulk mode density we can use periodic boundary conditions. Allowed wavevectors are separated by \(2\pi/L\) in each coordinate. Count a spherical shell in wavevector space, divide by the volume per state and include two independent transverse polarisations. Substituting \(k=2\pi\nu/c\) gives \(g(\nu)d\nu/V=8\pi\nu^2d\nu/c^3\). Using standing waves in an ideally reflecting large box gives the same leading volume density when directions and polarisations are counted consistently.

One oscillator mode of frequency \(\nu\) can hold \(m=0,1,2,\ldots\) photons. Its thermal occupation has probabilities proportional to \(e^{-mh\nu/k_BT}\). Summing the geometric series and taking its logarithmic derivative gives \(\langle m\rangle=1/(e^{h\nu/k_BT}-1)\). Multiplying by the density of modes and energy per photon gives spectral energy density \(u_\nu\). The zero-point contribution of a harmonic oscillator is not part of the thermal power exchanged between these black surfaces. At low frequency the result approaches an energy \(k_BT\) per mode; extending that classical limit to arbitrarily high frequency would make the integrated power diverge.

An isotropic distribution of energy travelling at speed \(c\) produces a normal flux through a small aperture weighted by the direction cosine. Per unit solid angle its radiance is \(B_\nu=cu_\nu/(4\pi)\). Transform to equal wavelength intervals using \(B_\lambda d\lambda=B_\nu|d\nu|\). The two useful spectral forms are

\[B_\nu=\frac{2h\nu^3}{c^2}\frac1{e^{h\nu/k_BT}-1},\]
\[B_\lambda=\frac{2hc^2}{\lambda^5}\frac1{e^{hc/(\lambda k_BT)}-1}.\]

The wavelength expression has units of watts per square metre per steradian per metre of wavelength. To express it per micrometre multiply by one millionth. At 400 K and 10 micrometres the result is 33.56 W per square metre per steradian per micrometre. At 295 K it is 9.14 in the same units. Equal wavelength and equal frequency bins carry different widths, so their distributions reach their maxima at different converted wavelengths. A spectrum's peak should always name its horizontal variable and spectral-density convention.

Distinguish emitter power from brightness in a chosen direction

For a black surface with angle-independent radiance into its outward hemisphere, integrate \(\cos\theta\,d\Omega\). The result is \(\pi\), not \(2\pi\). The emitted spectral power per unit area is \(\pi B_\lambda\). Integrate over frequency using \(x=h\nu/(k_BT)\). The remaining integral \(\int_0^\infty x^3/(e^x-1)\,dx=\pi^4/15\) follows by expanding \(1/(e^x-1)\) as a sum of exponentials and integrating term by term. This yields Stefan–Boltzmann exitance \(M=\sigma T^4\) and identifies the constant \(\sigma=2\pi^5k_B^4/(15h^3c^2)\).

A black patch of 1.0 square millimetre at 400 K emits about 1.45 mW into its entire outward hemisphere over all wavelengths. The same patch at 295 K emits about 0.429 mW. Replacing one by the other changes all-wavelength hemispherical power by about 1.02 mW. This is not the power a small infrared receiver sees through a narrow filter. The integrated emitter number is valuable for energy conservation, but by itself contains no receiver area, direction, passband or optical transmission.

The surface spectrum of a body in thermal equilibrium has directional spectral emissivity equal to its absorptivity in the corresponding reciprocal optical modes under the usual reciprocal-material conditions. An opaque diffuse grey surface with emissivity \(\epsilon\) in an isotropically illuminated background can be modelled by \(\epsilon B_\lambda(T)+(1-\epsilon)B_\lambda(T_{\rm bg})\). It emits less than a black surface but also reflects background. A shiny object viewed in a warm room may consequently display a radiance temperature nearer the reflected scene than its own physical temperature. Specular surfaces require explicit directional knowledge of that scene.

Derive the geometric acceptance without counting the angle twice

An area element \(dA\) normal to the collection axis emits into \(d\Omega\) at angle \(\theta\) a spectral power \(B_\lambda\cos\theta\,dA\,d\Omega\). For a uniformly filled cone of half-angle \(\alpha\) and a uniform patch of area \(A\), integrating gives \(A\pi\sin^2\alpha\). This is its geometrical throughput in air when there is no vignetting. Optical étendue more generally uses \(n^2dA\cos\theta\,d\Omega\). A lossless passive optical relay in geometrical optics preserves this phase-space measure; correspondingly \(L/n^2\) is conserved along a ray in transparent media under the usual stationary conditions.

A direct lens example makes the restriction tangible. An image relay enlarging a uniformly bright patch by a factor of two in each transverse direction forms an image four times the area. In a lossless paraxial design filling the original pupil, each image point then occupies one quarter the solid angle. A detector covering the whole enlarged image and accepting that cone can receive the original power. Placing a fixed small sensor at the image plane intercepts less area instead. Higher magnification has not created additional radiance, even if it makes a small detail easier to resolve.

For the same 1.0 square millimetre patch seen within ten degrees, the projected acceptance is \(9.47\times10^{-8}\) square metre steradian. A wavelength-independent combined transmission and detector absorption of 0.65 leaves effective acceptance \(6.16\times10^{-8}\) square metre steradian. Integrating the Planck curve from 8 to 12 micrometres gives source and background band radiances 133.74 and 35.43 W per square metre per steradian. Their difference yields only 6.05 microwatts in the receiver. Keeping a single midpoint radiance times four micrometres happens to be fairly close here, but there is no fixed universal midpoint correction across temperatures or filters.

Open the live experiment

Set source and ambient temperatures equal. The emitted and ambient curves remain finite while the differenced instrument reading vanishes. Increase the cone half-angle from ten to twenty degrees and predict the scale factor \(\sin^2 20^\circ/\sin^2 10^\circ=3.88\). The spectral shape should stay fixed if transmission is separable from angle. If real data shift spectral shape with aperture, inspect filter angle dependence, source nonuniformity and stray light. The integral in the readout is computed on the specified 8–12 micrometre band even though the graph shows wavelengths beyond it.

Convert received power to calibrated voltage or counts

A thermal detector may respond to absorbed power through a temperature rise and an electrical responsivity. A photon-counting detector measures absorbed photons, so its count rate contains a factor \(\lambda/(hc)\) multiplying spectral power before integration. Two receivers with identical geometric acceptance and nominal quantum efficiency can respond differently to a changing source temperature if one output is proportional to energy and the other to photon number. Calibrate the actual electrical transfer and make the time interval explicit for accumulated counts.

For an ideal linear power detector let \(V=V_0+gP\), with offset \(V_0\) and gain \(g\). Two calibrated sources spanning the use range determine both. A black reference port at the same ambient temperature can also reject a common background in a chopped measurement. A filter, window or warm lens that absorbs thermal radiation also emits it. If these optics remain unchanged and stable, part of their own emission cancels in a reference difference. A reference traversing a different path generally leaves a residual input that is not captured by an electronic dark measurement.

Suppose effective source-minus-ambient powers of 2.00 and 8.00 microwatts yield 0.120 and 0.420 V. The fitted slope is 0.0500 V per microwatt with an offset of 0.020 V. A reading of 0.320 V implies 6.00 microwatts after offset correction. Substituting the same reading directly into a single-point gain with zero assumed offset would bias the inferred temperature. Rechecking a third reference near six microwatts tests linearity, while repeated zero-input readings at realistic instrument temperature test offset drift.

Invert the radiance and propagate uncertainties

A narrowband blackbody radiance measurement can be inverted analytically by solving Planck's expression for \(T\). In a broad passband use numerical quadrature across the calibrated spectral response and solve a scalar monotone equation when emissivity and background are known. The derivative at a single wavelength is particularly informative for estimating precision. With \(x=hc/(\lambda k_BT)\),

\[\frac{\partial\log B_\lambda}{\partial\log T}=\frac{x}{1-e^{-x}},\]
\[T=\frac{hc/(\lambda k_B)}{\log[1+2hc^2/(\lambda^5B_\lambda)]}.\]

At 10 micrometres and 400 K the logarithmic sensitivity is 3.70. A one-percent relative radiance uncertainty therefore gives about 1.08 K temperature uncertainty if all other quantities are exact. An unknown five-percent emissivity error can dominate that estimate. A two-colour temperature ratio may reduce uncertainty from an emissivity that is constant across the two narrow bands, but cannot cancel an arbitrary spectral emissivity or a large reflected ambient component.

As an example consider a grey object at 400 K and ambient 295 K with emissivity 0.70. At ten micrometres its outgoing radiance is \(0.70(33.56)+0.30(9.14)=26.24\) in the earlier spectral units. Inverting as a perfect blackbody gives a brightness temperature of about 375 K. The difference is a physical radiance–temperature degeneracy. Repeat at known ambient temperatures while keeping the object thermally stabilised to constrain the reflected fraction. If heating the background changes the actual object temperature, a second independent temperature sensor or a transient thermal model is necessary.

When detector heat capacity sets the measured signal

Consider a thermal receiver of heat capacity \(C_d\) coupled to its surroundings with thermal conductance \(G_d\). For small excursions its temperature obeys \(C_d\dot\theta+G_d\theta=P_{\rm abs}(t)\), where \(\theta\) is the excess above its operating point. A voltage thermometer with responsivity \(r_T\) converts this into output. Its frequency-dependent power responsivity is \(r_T/(G_d-i\omega C_d)\). The geometric and spectral calculations determine absorbed optical power, while this thermal equation determines how much of its modulation appears at the electrical output.

Take \(C_d=2.0\) microjoules per kelvin and \(G_d=0.10\) milliwatt per kelvin. The thermal time constant is 20 ms and ordinary corner frequency is 7.96 Hz. A sinusoidal absorbed-power amplitude of 6.0 microwatts would give a static temperature rise of 0.060 K. At 20 Hz the amplitude is reduced by \(1/\sqrt{1+(2\pi f\tau)^2}=0.370\), leaving about 22.2 mK with a phase lag magnitude near 68 degrees. A receiver calibrated with a steady optical power is not automatically calibrated for this chopped experiment.

A square-wave chopper also changes the relation between time-domain peak differences and a lock-in's fundamental reading. For equal hot and reference dwell times, a square wave switching between powers \(P_1,P_0\) has fundamental sinusoidal peak amplitude \(2(P_1-P_0)/\pi\), with different factors for rms and peak reports. The thermal transfer attenuates this component at the chopper frequency and the odd harmonics at their respective frequencies. Substituting the full hot-minus-reference power directly into the first-harmonic response would overestimate the expected signal.

Measure a frequency sweep on a stable reference and test whether amplitude and phase share a single time constant. A geometrical error rescales the amplitude but ordinarily leaves this thermal phase unchanged. A second thermal mass or an electronic filter can introduce another pole. Lowering chop frequency increases thermal signal until the static limit but can expose low-frequency environmental drift. Choose the frequency by comparing the calibrated response with the measured noise spectrum.

Finally, a reference and an unknown source must illuminate the same active area if responsivity varies across the receiver. Imaging one source as a small spot and the other as a filled patch can create a calibration bias even when total spectral powers agree. Translating a reference spot across the detector or imaging the same aperture successively at both temperatures tests that spatial uniformity assumption.

If the sensor is used on a source much smaller than the calibrated reference aperture, record an image or a field-stop scan to determine its filling fraction. Otherwise even exact knowledge of filter transmission and thermodynamic temperature leaves an unknown geometrical scale factor. Aperture filling is part of the measurement equation, not an electronic gain setting.

Tutorial · Infer emissivity using two controlled backgrounds

An opaque diffuse sample at fixed temperature is observed at 10 micrometres against two isotropic backgrounds with calibrated spectral radiances 8.00 and 16.00 W per square metre per steradian per micrometre. The resulting outgoing readings are 25.00 and 27.00 in the same units. Infer emissivity and the sample's blackbody-equivalent radiance. Estimate the significance of independent reading errors of 0.10 units.

Separate the reflected and emitted contributions

The increased outgoing radiance is 2.00 for an 8.00 increase of background. Thus reflectivity is 0.250 and emissivity 0.750. The first measurement gives intrinsic blackbody radiance \([25.00-0.25(8.00)]/0.75=30.67\). The second returns the same value. With 0.10 independent uncertainty on each outgoing reading and exact backgrounds, the slope uncertainty is \(\sqrt2(0.10)/8=0.0177\), as is the absolute emissivity uncertainty. Larger background separation can improve this slope estimate if sample temperature stays fixed.

If the sample transmits radiation, the residual fraction does not all correspond to reflection. A controlled change behind the sample helps detect that additional path. A directional specular reflector exposed to a warm enclosure also does not necessarily see the same angular average used in the diffuse model. Without these checks, a formally precise fitted emissivity can describe the experimental surroundings instead of the material.

Tutorial · Design a numerical spectral-integration check

An engineer tabulates spectral radiance per micrometre on a wavelength mesh. Another rewrites the detector model on an equally spaced frequency mesh, obtaining a signal that differs by several percent after independent mesh refinement. Explain what must transform, what stays dimensionless and how to compare a photon-counting channel with a power channel.

Transform the complete measurement integral

The differential power \(B_\lambda\,d\lambda\) must equal \(B_\nu\,|d\nu|\), with \(|d\lambda/d\nu|=c/\nu^2\). A dimensionless transmission curve becomes the same physical transmission at \(\lambda=c/\nu\); it acquires no additional Jacobian. The integration bounds reverse when increasing wavelength is replaced by increasing frequency. A response specified per unit wavelength, or a calibrated gain integrating a particular finite bin, must instead be interpreted from how it was defined.

Evaluate a unit-transmission test using a very narrow band, then the full 8–12 micrometre band whose radiance values are given above. Refine each grid independently, treating sharp filter edges consistently and converting per-micrometre densities before using metres. For a photon counter divide each spectral power contribution by photon energy before summation. Replacing that energy by a single central-wavelength value is an approximation that should be checked against the full band and source temperature. Close agreement between two different meshes using the same incorrect density is not a valid units check.

NIST radiance-temperature calibrations · spectral radiance, sources and uncertainty

Moving-mirror Doppler metrology

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A moving mirror changes optical phase, shifts the reflected frequency and exchanges energy and momentum with the light. In many displacement measurements the classical phase rule is extraordinarily accurate. Derive its exact inertial-frame counterpart, identify which time a detector samples and find the practical uncertainties that usually matter well before a relativistic correction does.

Match phases on a boundary that moves

In a vacuum laboratory let an incident plane wave travel along \(+x\) with phase \(kx-\omega t\). A perfectly reflecting plane mirror has position \(X(t)\), receding for positive velocity. The reflected wave travels along \(-x\) with phase \(-k_r x-\omega_r t+\phi_0\). At the moving conducting surface the tangential electric fields must cancel in the mirror rest frame. For a constant mirror speed this requires equality of the two phase rates evaluated on its trajectory. The phase reversal at reflection supplies a constant offset and disappears on differentiation.

\[kv-\omega=-k_rv-\omega_r,\]
\[\frac{\omega_r}{\omega}=\frac{1-\beta}{1+\beta},\]
\[\beta=v/c .\]

Here \(k=\omega/c\) and \(k_r=\omega_r/c\) are positive magnitudes. A receding mirror redshifts the reflected light and an approaching mirror blueshifts it. Another derivation boosts into the mirror frame, where incident and reflected frequencies are equal, reverses the wavevector and boosts back. This yields a product of two Doppler factors, one for each transformation. Multiplying the first factor by its inverse without reversing the wavevector would predict no shift.

Define the signed beat \(f_b=f_i-f_r\). The exact and first-order relations with vacuum wavelength \(\lambda_0\) are

\[f_b=\frac{2v}{\lambda_0(1+\beta)},\]
\[f_b\simeq\frac{2v}{\lambda_0},\]
\[v=\frac{\lambda_0 f_b}{2-\lambda_0 f_b/c}.\]

At 633 nm and \(v=2.00\) mm/s the beat is 6319.12 Hz. The relative correction to the first-order expression is only \(6.67\times10^{-12}\). Frequency scale, geometry and air refractive index are ordinarily much larger uncertainties in an uncorrected bench experiment. The same exact expression makes a genuinely different prediction at \(\beta=0.10\): \(f_r/f_i=0.81818\), whereas the first-order frequency ratio is 0.80. This latter value is a theoretical benchmark for the formula rather than a required moving-bench experiment.

Connect reflection time with the detector's interference phase

Put an ideal stationary beamsplitter and detector at the origin, and let the source continuously illuminate the mirror along the measurement arm. A wave arriving back at laboratory time \(t\) was reflected at \(t_r\) satisfying \(t=t_r+X(t_r)/c\). It left the beamsplitter at \(t_0=t_r-X(t_r)/c\). Combining these gives \(t-t_0=2X(t_r)/c\). With a stable source and a fixed reference arm, the accumulated return phase relative to the reference is proportional to \(2kX(t_r)\) up to a constant and an electronic quadrature sign.

\[\phi(t)=\frac{4\pi X(t_r)}{\lambda_0}+\phi_{\rm off},\]
\[\frac1{2\pi}\frac{d\phi}{dt}=\frac{2v(t_r)}{\lambda_0[1+v(t_r)/c]} .\]

The denominator comes from differentiating the arrival-time condition. It is not obtained from differentiating a geometrical arm length at detector time while keeping the reflection time fixed. For very slow motion and a modest arm one can replace the retarded trajectory by the present one to high accuracy. At higher bandwidth a fixed path delay can still matter even if the velocity correction is tiny. A one-metre mirror distance gives roughly 3.34 ns from reflection event to detection.

A sinusoidally driven mirror with amplitude 100 nm and ordinary mechanical frequency 1.0 kHz has phase amplitude \(4\pi A/\lambda_0=1.99\) rad at 633 nm. The peak speed is 0.628 mm/s and peak signed beat about 1.99 kHz. The instantaneous beat changes sign at each mechanical turning point. A frequency counter returning a single unsigned average misses that directional information. Demodulating the phase or fitting the sinusoidal motion to both quadratures can recover position, speed and reversal times.

Infer displacement or signed speed without losing complete fringes

If stationary reference and returned fields overlap in polarisation, spectrum and space, their combined intensity contains a cross term proportional to \(\cos\phi\). A single intensity channel cannot distinguish increasing from decreasing phase at an intensity extremum. Two calibrated quadrature outputs ideally read \(X_s=V\cos\phi\), \(Y_s=V\sin\phi\) after removal of offsets and gain normalisation. Recover wrapped phase with the two-argument arctangent. Unwrap successive phase differences only when the sampling interval and noise allow the branch to be identified.

Open the live experiment

The signed velocity control reverses the direction traced by the quadratures. Hold its absolute value fixed and change sign. The cosine channel alone remains almost identical at laboratory speeds; the sine channel reverses the progression from its initial value. Reduce the visibility and keep in mind that the graph shows noise-free curves. With fixed electronic amplitude noise a smaller circle makes angular uncertainty larger, even though the exact underlying phase still advances at the same rate.

One full phase turn represents a physical mirror displacement of half the vacuum wavelength at normal incidence. A 20-fringe movement at 633 nm is 6.33 micrometres. If both endpoints retain phase residuals, add their signed difference to the complete turn count before converting to distance. A missed fringe biases displacement by 316.5 nm regardless of how accurately sub-fringe phases are fitted. For the default speed and wavelength the magnitude of frequency is 6.32 kHz. To unwrap a plain uniformly sampled phase with less than half a turn between adjacent samples one needs a sampling frequency above 12.64 kHz, and usually a margin for noise and acceleration.

Quadrature imbalance distorts the ideal circle into an ellipse shifted away from the voltage origin. Taking an arctangent of raw voltages then introduces a periodic position error. Record a slow multi-fringe calibration, fit channel offsets, scales and nonorthogonality, and test the recovered phase on held-out data with reversed motion. Calibration using only a tiny arc of the ellipse makes offset and scale poorly identifiable even if the residuals on that arc are small.

Close the energy and momentum balance at the mirror

One incident photon has energy \(hf_i\) and momentum \(+hf_i/c\). On reflection it has energy \(hf_r\) and momentum \(-hf_r/c\). The mirror acquires momentum \(h(f_i+f_r)/c\) and energy \(h(f_i-f_r)\). Their ratio is exactly \(v\) when the prescribed constant-speed reflection formula applies. A recoil-free moving boundary can exchange mechanical work \(v\,\Delta p\) while a drive maintains its velocity. For a freely recoiling low-mass mirror illuminated by a small number of photons the fixed-velocity approximation must be replaced by energy and momentum conservation for the joint system.

For an incident beam with power \(P_i\) crossing a stationary plane in the lab, the number of photons encountering the receding surface per laboratory time is \(P_i(1-\beta)/(hf_i)\). Multiply by transferred momentum per encounter to get the force on that moving surface. The result simplifies to \(F=(2P_i/c)(1-\beta)/(1+\beta)\). At rest, a 10 mW perfectly reflecting beam gives 66.7 pN. For a 2.0 mm/s receding mirror at that power, the corresponding mechanical work rate is only \(1.33\times10^{-13}\) W to leading order.

A detector fixed upstream sees reflected-wave intensity reduced by the square of the reflected frequency ratio for an ideal steady plane wave. Its received power change compared with a fixed-plane incident flux is not in general equal to the instantaneous work on the mirror. The region of field between the fixed reference plane and the moving surface grows or shrinks and stores energy. Instead compare powers crossing the moving boundary with their proper encounter rates, or account for the change of field energy in a specified control volume. For a finite pulse the integrated photon count is unchanged under perfect reflection and the total energy loss per photon is the Doppler shift already derived.

Estimate realistic errors before pursuing small corrections

At normal incidence in vacuum the first-order speed estimate is \(\lambda_0 f_b/2\). For a beam at angle \(\theta\) to the translation direction, with an appropriately arranged reflection/return geometry interrogating mirror-normal motion, the usual small-speed sensitivity along the normal includes \(\cos\theta\). A tilt of two degrees gives a relative cosine factor 0.99939, a 610 parts-per-million reduction. A plane mirror tilted relative to a fixed coaxial receiver can additionally send the reflected beam outside the collection path, so specify how oblique reflected and reference fields are recombined. Blindly interpreting reduced fringe visibility as a velocity correction conflates these two effects.

In a homogeneous stationary transparent medium a quasistatic phase measurement measures optical path \(nL\). If \(n\) changes during a displacement measurement, the phase derivative includes both \(n\dot L\) and \(L\dot n\) when the simple uniform-medium approximation holds. A refractive-index change of one part in a million over a 0.5 m arm imitates about 0.50 micrometres of path-length change. A short differential arm, a refractive-index reference or a controlled environment can separate it from actual motion. The vacuum relativistic formula cannot simply absorb an arbitrary time-varying medium by replacing \(c\) everywhere with \(c/n\).

For a phase-difference velocity estimate over a record of duration \(T\), the displacement noise from two independent endpoint phase errors of standard deviation \(s_\phi\) is \(\lambda_0\sqrt2s_\phi/(4\pi)\). At 633 nm, \(s_\phi=0.010\) rad and \(T=2\) ms, the resulting velocity uncertainty is about \(0.356\) micrometres per second. Fitting a slope over many samples can improve white-noise precision, but laser drift, mechanical acceleration and missed branches require their own models. Report the time interval over which a constant velocity is being claimed.

Recover a small vibration amplitude from optical sidebands

A phase-sensitive receiver is not the only way to recover periodic motion. Let a mirror vibrate at angular frequency \(\Omega\) about a fixed mean, with displacement \(A\cos\Omega t\) and speed sufficiently small for a first-order optical path description. The reflected analytic signal relative to its carrier contains \(e^{im\cos\Omega t}\) with modulation index \(m=4\pi A/\lambda_0\). Expand the exponential in its Fourier series. The power of sideband order \(j\) relative to the incident unmodulated carrier is \(J_j(m)^2\), where \(J_j\) is a Bessel function. Energy conservation of the phase factor requires that all the sideband powers sum to one in this phase-only approximation.

For a 10.0 nm displacement amplitude at 633 nm the index is 0.1985. To leading small-index order, each first sideband carries \((m/2)^2=0.00985\) of the original carrier power. The central component loses approximately twice this amount. A receiver collecting only one sideband would infer the wrong vibration amplitude if it treated that signal as the sum of both. At larger amplitudes higher Bessel orders become important and a single sideband amplitude is no longer a monotone function of displacement. A multi-sideband fit or an independent coarse amplitude resolves the ambiguity.

With a balanced optical heterodyne reference offset far enough from zero frequency, both sidebands can be separated electronically from the carrier beat. Measuring their power ratio to the central beat cancels a common optical gain, but any electronic response slope between their frequencies needs calibration. Pure phase modulation predicts symmetric sideband powers. Asymmetry can arise from simultaneous amplitude modulation or an unequal frequency response rather than from a change in mirror speed. Measuring their complex phases distinguishes amplitude and phase modulation more directly.

A useful consistency check uses three independent measured quantities. Infer displacement amplitude from the first two sidebands, obtain the mechanical frequency from their separation and predict peak Doppler beat from the product of those two values. Then compare with a separate unwrapped quadrature phase trace. For a rigid sinusoidal mirror these should agree within timing and wavelength uncertainties. An interferometric signal containing two different moving surfaces can fit a few sideband powers yet fail the predicted time-dependent phase; changing the illuminated spot or one surface position tests that possibility. A spectrum and a time trace jointly constrain motion in a way that a single fringe visibility cannot.

Tutorial · Resolve a branch ambiguity in a vibrating mirror

A mirror returns a signed mean beat of 8.0 kHz while moving uniformly over a short time window. The vacuum wavelength is 632.8 nm. The detector samples quadrature at 10.0 kHz and a naive nearest-branch unwrap reports minus 2.0 kHz. Explain the discrepancy, infer both candidate speeds and design a measurement to select the correct one.

Track the sampled phase and use an independent constraint

A true phase advance of 0.80 cycle per sample is indistinguishable from minus 0.20 cycle per sample when only consecutive wrapped angles are available. The corresponding physical speeds are plus 2.531 mm/s and minus 0.6328 mm/s. Both give exactly the same ideal complex samples at that sampling rate under the first-order laboratory formula. A smaller least-squares residual on one constant-speed branch cannot resolve the ambiguity without another observation.

Increase the sampling frequency above 16 kHz with margin or use an independently known direction and bounded speed range that excludes alternative aliases. A second wavelength also changes the beat while preserving physical velocity and can resolve the branch if the two ambiguous frequency ladders do not coincide over the admissible range. For accelerated motion verify the condition at the maximum instantaneous beat rather than at its signed average over a complete vibration period.

Tutorial · Can this beat measurement verify a relativistic correction?

A translation standard operates at 1.00 m/s and an interferometer at 500 nm records a nominal 4.00 MHz beat. Estimate the difference between exact and first-order vacuum predictions. A timebase has a fractional uncertainty \(10^{-8}\), and wavelength is uncertain by \(2\times10^{-8}\). Assess the test and state one way to reject an offset without confusing it with the desired correction.

Calculate the small difference without subtracting nearly equal optical frequencies

Use the exact beat formula or expand it directly. The correction is approximately \(-2v^2/(\lambda_0c)=-0.0133\) Hz for the receding mirror. The stated timebase contributes 0.040 Hz and the wavelength scale contributes 0.080 Hz to a 4 MHz prediction, even before including the velocity standard or alignment. This setup does not resolve the correction with those absolute uncertainties. Subtracting two floating-point optical frequencies near hundreds of terahertz is also a poor numerical route to such a small difference.

Reverse the velocity with equal magnitude. The leading signed beat is odd in velocity, while its leading relativistic correction is even. Adding the two signed beats cancels the ideal leading term and leaves about minus 0.0267 Hz. This reversal also preserves a fixed electronic frequency offset, which would add twice to the same sum. Take stationary-mirror reference readings and vary the speed magnitude; the wanted even term scales as speed squared. Different arm alignments or asymmetric velocity calibration during reversal can still imitate part of that effect.

Cambridge Electromagnetism · wave propagation, radiation momentum and Lorentz transformations

Where momentum waits between source and receiver

Electrodynamics

Derive the electromagnetic momentum balance, trace light pressure through a finite pulse, and account for the angular impulse when a magnetic flux decays.

Give a region a mechanical and a field account

Charges respond to Lorentz force density \(\mathbf f=\rho\mathbf E+\mathbf J\times\mathbf B\). To predict the total force on enclosed matter, one can measure the fields on a surrounding vacuum surface. First eliminate the sources. In vacuum Gauss’s law gives \(\rho=\epsilon_0\nabla\cdot\mathbf E\), and the Ampère–Maxwell law gives \(\mathbf J=(\nabla\times\mathbf B)/\mu_0-\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E\). Introduce \(\mathbf g=\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\mathbf B=\mathbf S/c^2\). Its time derivative supplies both \(\epsilon_0\partial_t\mathbf E\times\mathbf B\) and \(\epsilon_0\mathbf E\times\partial_t\mathbf B\). The first cancels the displacement-current term in the force; Faraday’s law rewrites the second.

For example, the electric terms now read \(\epsilon_0[\mathbf E(\nabla\cdot\mathbf E)-\mathbf E\times(\nabla\times\mathbf E)]\). Component \(i\) is \(\partial_j[\epsilon_0(E_iE_j-\delta_{ij}E^2/2)]\), with repeated Cartesian indices summed. The magnetic terms have the same form because \(\nabla\cdot\mathbf B=0\). Integration gives

\[T_{ij}=\epsilon_0(E_iE_j-\tfrac12 E^2\delta_{ij})+\frac1{\mu_0}(B_iB_j-\tfrac12 B^2\delta_{ij}),\]
\[\mathbf F_{\rm matter}=\oint T\mathbf n\,dA-\frac d{dt}\int\mathbf g\,dV .\]

The normal points out of the chosen volume. Positive \(T_{xx}\) denotes tension along \(x\) in this convention. For a wave travelling in \(+x\) with transverse fields, \(T_{xx}=-u\), where \(u\) is energy density. On an inlet whose outward normal is \(-\hat{\mathbf x}\), the normal traction therefore points in \(+x\).

Measure the time before light reaches a detector

A finite plane pulse in vacuum has \(|\mathbf S|=cu\). Its total momentum is \(U/c\) along the propagation direction. Surround a freely absorbing target and a length of initially empty space upstream with a vacuum control surface. While a pulse enters but has yet to reach the target, the inlet stress integral is nonzero. The electromagnetic momentum inside grows at exactly the same rate. The minus time derivative in the total force cancels the inlet contribution and the target has zero force during this interval.

Worked optical impulse

A 0.60 mJ pulse is fully absorbed after crossing the inlet. Initially and finally the region contains no pulse. Integrating the balance over that full time removes the stored-field term. The target gains \(0.60\times10^{-3}/c=2.00\times10^{-12}\) N s. For ideal normal reflection the exiting pulse carries the opposite momentum, giving twice that impulse. For a transparent window with parallel faces at normal incidence the outgoing pulse may eventually have the same vacuum momentum as the incoming pulse, while the two faces still experience transient forces.

Convert distributed field momentum into a turning impulse

Now take a long coaxial arrangement: an inner narrow cylindrical shell of radius \(a\) carries free charge \(+\lambda\) per length; an outer shell of radius \(b\) carries \(-\lambda\). There is a uniform axial magnetic field \(B_0\) over the annular gap, with end effects negligible. For a definite preparation take an ideal long solenoid producing uniform field throughout \(r<b\), and confine the initial electric field to the gap. Between shells \(E_r=\lambda/(2\pi\epsilon_0 r)\). The field momentum density points towards negative azimuth because \(\hat{\mathbf r}\times\hat{\mathbf z}=-\hat{\boldsymbol\phi}\).

\[g_\phi=-\lambda B_0/(2\pi r),\]
\[L_{{\rm field},z}'=\int_a^b r g_\phi\,2\pi r\,dr=-\tfrac12\lambda B_0(b^2-a^2).\]

The prime denotes angular momentum per length. While the coil current decays slowly, Faraday’s law gives \(2\pi r E_\phi=-\pi r^2\dot B\) for radii up to \(b\). The torque on each shell per length is \(r\lambda_{\rm shell}E_\phi(r)\). Integrating from \(B_0\) to zero yields \(+\lambda B_0a^2/2\) on the inner shell and \(-\lambda B_0b^2/2\) on the outer. Their sum reproduces the initial field angular momentum. Clamps would receive those impulses if the shells were held fixed.

Worked sign and magnitude

With \(\lambda=20\) nC/m, \(B_0=0.25\) T, \(a=10\) mm and \(b=30\) mm, the initial \(L'_z=-2.0\times10^{-12}\) kg m/s. That is angular momentum per metre of apparatus. The outer shell receives nine times the inner impulse magnitude and rotates in the opposite sense. Torque from the induced azimuthal electric field supplies this transfer even though both shells started at rest.

Transfer angular momentum from a field into two shells

The inner radius is 10 mm and its line charge is +20 nC/m. For a 30 mm outer radius and an initial field of 0.25 T, the final shell impulses are +0.25 and −2.25 in units of 10⁻¹² kg m/s. Trace the four accounts during switch-off.

Choose a control surface for momentum

Find a pressure when transmission and reflection coexist

A normal beam of intensity 300 W/m² reaches a free-standing film in vacuum. Reflected power fraction is 0.20 and transmitted fraction is 0.50. Find the time-averaged pressure and identify the momentum carried away.

Work through the solution

With incident direction positive, outgoing momentum per area and time is \((T-R)I/c\). The deposited amount is \((1-T+R)I/c=0.70(300)/c=0.700\,\mu{\rm Pa}\). The absorbed part contributes \(0.30I/c\) and reflection adds \(0.40I/c\). The result assumes forward and backward beams remain normal to the film.

Reverse just the charges

In the coaxial example reverse both shell charges while keeping the axial field and current decay unchanged. Which quantities reverse?

Work through the solution

The radial field, stored azimuthal momentum and angular momentum reverse. Faraday’s induced \(E_\phi\) follows the same history because the flux decay is unchanged. Each electric force and angular impulse reverses since the charge signs reverse. This independent sign argument checks the cross product in the original momentum density.

Practicum · audit a pulse bouncing inside a partly absorbing cavity

A shutter has just prepared a short \(U_0=1.20\) mJ pulse travelling to the right in an otherwise empty one-dimensional cavity. Start the accounting after preparation. The left mirror reflects perfectly. At every encounter with the right mirror 60% of incident pulse energy is reflected, 30% passes into vacuum on the right, and 10% is absorbed. Pulse duration is short compared with the flight time across the cavity. Mirrors are mounted on massive force probes; neglect their displacement and delay any re-emission of heat. The challenge is to reconcile two large, oppositely signed readings with a small net impulse.

Audit one encounter and decide which surface terms matter

For energy \(U\) just before an encounter with the right mirror, calculate its mechanical impulse. Then find the impulse when the reflected packet hits the left mirror. Which momentum is still present between those events?

Follow the calculation and check the result

The incoming momentum is \(+U/c\). The two outgoing packets carry \(+0.30U/c\) and \(-0.60U/c\). Momentum balance therefore assigns the right mirror \(+(1-0.30+0.60)U/c=+1.30U/c\). This includes reversing the reflected packet and stopping the absorbed packet. The left mirror receives \(-2(0.60U)/c=-1.20U/c\) at the next collision. Between those events the cavity still contains \(-0.60U/c\) of field momentum. The complete system also contains the \(+0.30U/c\) transmitted packet outside the cavity.

Stop the movie at a specified time

Freeze the experiment just after the third right-mirror encounter and before the next left-mirror encounter. Find the remaining energy, radiated output, deposited heat, both probe impulses, and total field momentum.

Follow the calculation and check the result

Three incident energies in units of \(U_0\) form the sequence \(1,0.6,0.36\). Their sum is 1.96. The output energy is \(0.30(1.96)U_0=0.588U_0\), heat is \(0.196U_0\), and \(0.216U_0\) remains. The three fractions sum to unity. Right-probe impulse is \(+1.30(1.96)U_0/c=+2.548U_0/c\). Only two left reflections have occurred, so the left probe reads \(-2(0.60+0.36)U_0/c=-1.920U_0/c\). Their sum is \(+0.628U_0/c\).

The escaped packets carry \(+0.588U_0/c\), but the packet still inside carries \(-0.216U_0/c\). Including these two contributions gives measured plus field momentum \((0.628+0.588-0.216)U_0/c=U_0/c\), the initial value. Counting a third left-hand reflection at this time would violate the preselected event boundary. Since \(U_0/c=4.00\times10^{-12}\) N s, the probe readings are approximately \(+10.2\) and \(-7.68\) pN s.

Sum the full history in two independent ways

Continue until no macroscopic pulse remains. Find the impulse on each mirror, then derive their combined reading directly from exterior measurements without examining any reflection.

Follow the calculation and check the result

The geometric series of right hits sums to \(U_0/(1-0.6)=2.5U_0\). The right impulse is \(3.25U_0/c\). Summing every left hit gives \(-2(0.60)U_0/[(1-0.60)c]=-3.00U_0/c\). Thus the pair receives \(+0.25U_0/c\). All transmitted packets travel right and together carry \(0.30U_0/(1-0.60)=0.75U_0\). An external vacuum control surface therefore gives \((1-0.75)U_0/c\) immediately. The absorbed \(0.25U_0\) accounts for the energy stored as heat.

A detector without enough time resolution can recover the integrated exterior momentum even though it cannot resolve the alternating internal forces. Conversely, two large internal probe signals can coexist with a modest net exterior impulse. In a numerical time march record separate signed transfers and the energy still in flight after each boundary update, then vary the stopping time. A budget that closes only after the very last encounter cannot diagnose an intermediate sign mistake.

Further reading

  • Griffiths, §§8.2.2–8.2.4, stress, momentum and angular momentum
  • Tong, Electromagnetism and Relativity, energy-momentum and angular momentum

Why a small transparent sphere scatters light

Electrodynamics

A particle can redistribute light without consuming it. The calculation starts with an electrostatic boundary-value problem and ends with power crossing a faraway sphere. Connecting the two limits predicts a wavelength dependence that can be tested with colour.

Find the induced dipole by matching two potentials

Take a sphere of radius \(a\) and relative permittivity \(\epsilon_r\) in vacuum. Its size must be small compared with the wavelength everywhere, including inside the particle. Across its diameter, the incident wave then looks almost like a uniform electric field \(E_0\hat{\mathbf z}\). Spherical symmetry and the applied field single out the angular factor \(\cos\theta\). Choose \(\phi_{\rm in}=-Cr\cos\theta\) and \(\phi_{\rm out}=-E_0r\cos\theta+D\cos\theta/r^2\). Continuity of tangential electric field is equivalent here to continuity of potential. With zero free surface charge, the normal displacement is also continuous. At \(r=a\),

\[C=E_0-D/a^3,\]
\[\epsilon_r C=E_0+2D/a^3.\]

Eliminate \(C\) to obtain \(D=a^3E_0(\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\). The coefficient of the dipole potential is \(p/(4\pi\epsilon_0)\). Hence \(p=\alpha_0 E_0\) with \(\alpha_0=4\pi\epsilon_0a^3(\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)\). The internal field \(3E_0/(\epsilon_r+2)\) explains the denominator. For \(\epsilon_r=1\) the disturbance vanishes. In the perfectly polarizable limit the internal field tends to zero.

Match a small dipole to an outgoing wave

Use complex amplitudes with time dependence \(e^{-i\omega t}\). The far electric field is \((k^2/4\pi\epsilon_0 r)[(\hat{\mathbf n}\times\mathbf p)\times\hat{\mathbf n}]e^{ikr}\), \(k=\omega/c\). Squaring its amplitude and multiplying by \(\epsilon_0 c/2\) gives the mean intensity. Divide by the incident intensity \(\epsilon_0c|E_0|^2/2\). The differential scattering cross section is then \(k^4|\alpha|^2\sin^2\theta/(16\pi^2\epsilon_0^2)\), with \(\theta\) measured from the dipole axis. Integrating the angular factor gives \(8\pi/3\):

\[\sigma_{\rm sca}=\frac{k^4|\alpha|^2}{6\pi\epsilon_0^2}\simeq\frac{8\pi}3 k^4a^6\left|\frac{\epsilon_r-1}{\epsilon_r+2}\right|^2 .\]

The sixth power originates in the coherent addition of dipoles throughout a volume proportional to \(a^3\). The fourth power in frequency comes from the second time derivative of the dipole in the radiative electric field. A cross section has units of area and measures removed power divided by incident intensity.

Where the scattered energy comes from

The incident field does work at mean rate \(\langle\mathbf E\cdot\dot{\mathbf p}\rangle=(\omega/2)\operatorname{Im}(\alpha)|E_0|^2\). Dividing by incident intensity yields \(\sigma_{\rm ext}=k\operatorname{Im}(\alpha)/\epsilon_0\). A strictly real electrostatic \(\alpha_0\) would predict no extracted power while predicting positive scattered power. The static calculation captured the leading dipole amplitude but omitted its small radiation-induced phase delay. For a lossless electric dipole its leading radiative correction satisfies

\[\frac1\alpha=\frac1{\alpha_0}-i\frac{k^3}{6\pi\epsilon_0}.\]

Indeed \(\operatorname{Im}\alpha=k^3|\alpha|^2/(6\pi\epsilon_0)\), making extinction equal to scattering. In an absorbing particle their difference is absorbed power per incident intensity. Finite-size dispersion and higher multipoles require additional terms once \(ka\) grows.

Size, colour and the limits of inference

A particle doubles in radius

For dilute spheres of the same weakly dispersive material, doubling \(a\) gives \(2^6=64\) times the scattered power per particle at fixed incident intensity. If the total volume of dielectric is held fixed, the number of spheres falls by eight. The whole sample then scatters eight times the power, provided phases between different particles are effectively uncorrelated and multiple scattering is negligible. The two questions describe different samples.

A frequency-independent contrast

For \(a=20\) nm, \(\epsilon_r=2.25\) and vacuum wavelength \(600\) nm, \(ka=0.209\) and \((\epsilon_r-1)/(\epsilon_r+2)=0.2941\). The leading cross section is \(5.58\times10^{-19}\) m², around \(4.4\times10^{-4}\) of the geometric area. At \(450\) nm the model predicts 3.16 times that cross section. A large discrepancy can indicate dispersion, additional multipoles, or a distribution of particle sizes.

Compare the scattering of several particle sizes

For radius 20 nm, relative permittivity 2.25 and wavelength 600 nm, the predicted cross section is about 0.56 nm². Halve the radius and estimate the drop before reading the plot. The curve uses a frequency-independent dielectric contrast.

Problem sheet · inverse measurements

Estimate a radius ratio

Two dilute suspensions have the same number density, composition and optical path length. In the single-scattering regime one produces twice the scattered intensity at the same angle. Infer the ratio of particle radii.

Work through the solution

The proportionality to \(a^6\) gives \(a_2/a_1=2^{1/6}=1.122\). A 12.2% change in radius doubles the signal. If counts or material contrasts are unknown, the same intensity change has multiple explanations.

Recover Thomson scattering

A free electron is driven by a weak field. Use \(m\ddot x=-eE_0e^{-i\omega t}\) to find its dipole and cross section. Why does the wavelength law change?

Work through the solution

The displacement amplitude is \(eE_0/(m\omega^2)\). Multiplying by electronic charge gives \(\alpha=-e^2/(m\omega^2)\). The \(\omega^{-4}\) in \(|\alpha|^2\) cancels \(k^4\). The cross section becomes \((8\pi/3)[e^2/(4\pi\epsilon_0mc^2)]^2\). This classical approximation assumes nonrelativistic electron motion and negligible recoil.

Further reading

  • Fließbach, chapter 25 · scattering of light
  • Cambridge Electromagnetism, chapter 5 · Rayleigh and Thomson scattering

From wavefronts to rays, focusing and caustics

Electrodynamics

A ray is a prediction extracted from the phase of a wave. Following its amplitude at the same time reveals the point at which a ray calculation loses its validity. A gently graded transparent material gives a solvable example of both effects.

Derive the phase and transport equations together

In a lossless isotropic dielectric with a slowly varying refractive index \(n(\mathbf r)\), consider one scalar polarisation component satisfying \(\nabla^2\psi+k_0^2 n^2\psi=0\), where \(k_0=\omega/c\). This scalar description works when vector-polarisation coupling is negligible over the scales of interest. Set \(\psi=A(\mathbf r)e^{ik_0S(\mathbf r)}\) with a slowly varying real amplitude. Differentiation twice gives

\[0=\nabla^2 A+i k_0[2\nabla A\cdot\nabla S+A\nabla^2S]+k_0^2 A(n^2-|\nabla S|^2).\]

If the material and amplitude vary little over a wavelength, first equate the leading \(k_0^2\) term to zero. At the next order the \(k_0\) term vanishes. The two results are the eikonal equation and transport equation:

\[|\nabla S|^2=n^2,\]
\[\nabla\cdot(A^2\nabla S)=0 .\]

The first fixes the optical length accumulated between neighbouring wavefronts. The second transports energy down a thin ray tube. Across a tube of area \(\mathcal A\) perpendicular to the phase gradient, \(A^2 n\mathcal A\) is constant to this order. In a uniform medium a spherical tube grows as \(r^2\), giving \(A\propto1/r\), consistent with the outgoing wave.

Follow the normal to a wavefront

Choose arclength \(s\) along the ray and \(\hat{\mathbf t}=\nabla S/n\). In the eikonal equation take a spatial derivative. Since \(\nabla S\) is a gradient, \((\nabla S\cdot\nabla)\nabla S=\nabla(|\nabla S|^2)/2=n\nabla n\). Divide by \(n\). The ray equation is

\[\frac{d}{ds}(n\hat{\mathbf t})=\nabla n .\]

A transverse increase of index bends the ray toward larger optical index. If \(n\) depends only on the coordinate normal to layers, the component of \(n\hat{\mathbf t}\) parallel to them stays constant. Thus \(n\sin\theta\) is conserved for an angle measured from the normal, recovering Snell's law. The same equation follows by requiring stationary optical length \(\int n\,ds\) when varying the path with fixed endpoints.

Solve a graded-index profile

For paraxial rays travelling close to \(+z\), take \(n(y)\simeq n_0(1-g^2y^2/2)\). Assume \(|gy|\ll1\) and \(|dy/dz|\ll1\). Then \(ds\simeq dz\), \(\hat t_y\simeq y'\), and \(d(ny')/dz\simeq n_0y''\) to leading order. Since \(\partial_y n=-n_0g^2y\), the trajectory solves \(y''+g^2y=0\). Initial height \(y_0\) and slope \(s_0\) give

\[y(z)=y_0\cos(gz)+\frac{s_0}{g}\sin(gz),\]
\[y'(z)=-g y_0\sin(gz)+s_0\cos(gz).\]

For a collimated bundle, all \(s_0=0\). The transverse magnification \(\partial y/\partial y_0=\cos(gz)\) vanishes simultaneously for all rays at \(z_f=\pi/(2g)\). This is a caustic of the ray description. In this planar example the ray density grows as \(1/|\cos(gz)|\). Its divergence signals that the neglected \(\nabla^2 A\) becomes important. The underlying wave stays finite and develops diffraction near the focus.

A focus and a reconstructed gradient

Refocus an off-axis bundle

Take \(g=20\ {\rm m^{-1}}\), initial heights between \(-1\) and \(+1\) mm and zero slopes. The predicted first focus is \(78.5\) mm downstream. The extreme rays acquire slopes of magnitude \(0.020\) at the focus, well within the paraxial regime. Tilt every initial ray by 0.010 rad. At \(z_f\) the common height shifts to \(s_0/g=0.50\) mm. The focal length is insensitive to this common tilt in the parabolic approximation.

Estimate the index from a photograph

Two successive crossings of initially parallel symmetric rays are 120 mm apart. Crossings occur at \(gz=\pi/2+m\pi\), so \(g=\pi/0.120=26.2\ {\rm m^{-1}}\). At a height of 2 mm, the predicted fractional index decrease from the central index is \(g^2y^2/2=1.37\times10^{-3}\). Resolving this small contrast through repeated bending can be easier than resolving it through a single refraction measurement.

Focus and tilt a bundle of paraxial rays

At gradient 20 m⁻¹, five parallel rays meet after 78.5 mm. A common tilt of 10 mrad displaces their meeting point by 0.50 mm. Follow the bundle through a crossing and decide why the geometric density ceases to predict a finite intensity there.

Problem sheet · optical path and diffraction

A travel-time check

A ray passes through two homogeneous layers of thicknesses \(a,b\), indices \(n_1,n_2\), with lateral endpoint separation \(D\). Write travel time as a function of crossing coordinate \(x\). Recover the interface condition.

Work through the solution

The time is \(T(x)=[n_1\sqrt{a^2+x^2}+n_2\sqrt{b^2+(D-x)^2}]/c\). The derivative vanishes when \(n_1 x/\sqrt{a^2+x^2}=n_2(D-x)/\sqrt{b^2+(D-x)^2}\). The two fractions are the sines of the angles to the interface normal. Each segment's length has positive second derivative, making this stationary path a local minimum in this geometry.

Estimate the diffraction scale

A circular pupil of numerical aperture 0.030 focuses vacuum light of wavelength 600 nm. Estimate the first dark-ring radius and compare it with a ray focus.

Work through the solution

For a uniformly illuminated circular aperture in the paraxial limit the first Airy minimum is \(0.61\lambda_0/\mathrm{NA}\), giving \(12.2\) μm. Different pupil shapes change the numerical factor. The wavelength dependence is absent from the ray crossing, so a ray trace alone cannot distinguish two experiments with identical geometry but different wavelengths.

  • Fließbach, chapter 36 · interference and diffraction
